## st-0005 — Numbers in the Night
Kaynak: ozgun

### A1
Sam lives with his father. Their house is old and small. In the house there is an old phone. It is on the wall by the door. No one calls it any more.

One night at nine, the phone sounds. Sam answers it. Someone says numbers.  "One hundred," says the man. Then the call ends. Sam looks at the phone a long time.

The next night, the phone sounds again. It is nine again. The same man says: "Ninety-nine." The night after that: "Ninety-eight." Every night the number goes down.

Sam tells his father. "It is a work call," says his father. "Or someone plays with you." But Sam writes every number on paper. He understands the numbers. They count down to something.

One night, Sam asks a question. "Who are you?" he says. The man is quiet for a little time. Then he says: "I am far away." "I am coming home." "Please wait for me."

The numbers go down, night after night. Ten. Nine. Eight. Sam waits by the phone every night. "Three," says the man. "Two." Then comes the last night.

At nine, the phone says nothing. Sam waits and waits. Then a big light goes over the town. It is white and very beautiful. It goes down slowly, like a star. Then it is night again.

The phone makes one small sound. Sam answers it fast. "I am home," says the man. "Thank you for waiting." The calls never come again. Now Sam looks at the night a lot. And sometimes he smiles.

### A2
Sam lived with his father in an old house. On the wall by the door there was an old phone. It came with the house, black and heavy. Nobody called it any more. "It is older than me," his father liked to say.

One night at exactly nine, the old phone started to sound. Sam and his father looked at each other. Sam answered it first. A man's voice said one word: "One hundred." Then the line went quiet. The voice sounded very far away, and a little tired.

The next night at nine, the phone sounded again. "Ninety-nine," said the same far voice. The night after that, it said: "Ninety-eight." It never said anything more, and it never missed a night. His father said it was a game or a work machine. But he started to stand near the phone at nine too.

Sam wrote every number in a little book. After two weeks, he understood it. The numbers were counting down to something. But to what? He looked at the night from his window and thought about it.

One night, Sam spoke before the voice could. "Who are you?" he asked. "What are the numbers?" The line was quiet for a long moment. "I am very far away," the voice said at last. "I have been away for a very long time." "I am coming home. Please wait for me." Then it said: "Forty-one," and the line went quiet.

After that, Sam never missed a call. He sat by the phone every night with his little book. The voice sounded closer now, night by night. "Ten," it said one evening, and Sam's heart went fast. "Three. Two. One." Then came the night of zero.

At nine, the phone stayed quiet. Sam and his father waited by the window without talking. At ten past nine, a great white light crossed over the town. It came down slowly, like a star that had found its place. The glasses on the table sang a little song. Then the night was black and quiet again.

A minute later, the old phone made one soft sound. Sam answered before it could make another. "I am home," said the voice, and it sounded very near now. "Thank you for waiting. Thank you for answering." The old phone never sounded again after that night. But Sam still looks at the night sky, and sometimes he smiles.

### B1
The old phone came with the house, like the doors and the garden. It hung on the wall by the kitchen, black and heavy and silent. Sam's father kept it because throwing it away felt somehow wrong. "It is older than both of us together," he used to say. Nobody had called it in years, and nobody knew its number. Sam liked its weight and its patient face, the way you like a sleeping animal.

That changed on a Tuesday night at exactly nine o'clock. The old phone rang so suddenly that Sam dropped his fork. He reached it first and lifted the heavy black handle. A man's voice, distant and tired, said a single word: "One hundred." Then the line clicked and went dead. Sam stood there holding the phone long after the sound had ended.

The call returned the next night at nine, and every night after. "Ninety-nine," the voice said. Then "ninety-eight", then "ninety-seven". It never explained, never answered, never missed a single night. His father tried tracing the number, but the line showed no caller at all. "A machine somewhere," he decided. "A broken computer counting to nothing." But Sam noticed that his father was always near the phone at nine.

Sam kept a notebook and recorded every number with its date. The pattern was perfect: one number less each night, no exceptions. If the counting continued, it would reach zero in early spring. Something was going to happen then, he was sure of it. He began watching the night sky from his window, though he could not say why. He told his best friend nothing; some things ask to be kept.

In February, Sam finally found the courage to interrupt. "Who are you?" he asked quickly, before the number could come. The silence lasted so long that he thought the line had died. "I am very far away," the voice said finally, and it sounded almost happy. "Further than you can imagine. I have been traveling home for a long time." "Your phone is the only one that still remembers how to listen." "Please keep answering. It helps me find the way. Forty-one."

After that night, Sam treated nine o'clock as a promise. He answered through school nights, sick nights, and one long power cut. The voice grew slowly clearer, as if the distance itself were shrinking. His father stopped calling it a broken machine and started making tea for two at nine. At school, Sam counted hours; at home, he counted nights. "Ten," the voice said one evening, and neither of them slept well. Three nights later it said: "One." And then came the night of zero.

At nine o'clock, for the first time in months, the phone stayed silent. Sam and his father stood at the window, watching the dark without a word. At ten past nine, a white light crossed the sky above the town. It moved slowly and came down beyond the hills, soft as a falling star. The windows sang quietly in their frames, and the night went still again. Far away a dog called once, and then even the dogs went quiet.

One minute later, the old phone rang one soft, short ring. "I am home," said the voice, and it sounded like someone smiling. "Thank you for waiting. Thank you for every single answer." The line went quiet forever after that, and the phone never rang again. They never learned who he was, or what had come down beyond the hills. But on clear nights, Sam still watches the sky, and he always will.

### B2
The phone had been hanging on the kitchen wall since before Sam was born. It was a heavy black instrument from another age, kept the way some families keep a grandfather's chair. The line still worked, technically, though nobody had dialed the number in living memory. Sam's father maintained that old machines deserved to retire in place, not in boxes. So it hung there, polished and silent, a small museum of missed conversations. Visitors sometimes asked why they kept it, and his father always gave the same answer. "Because it still works," he would say, "and working things deserve a wall."

It rang for the first time on a Tuesday in November, at nine o'clock exactly. The sound was so unexpected that Sam's father knocked his cup off the table. Sam reached the phone first and lifted the cold, heavy handle to his ear. Through a soft storm of distance and static, a man's voice pronounced one word: "One hundred." The line clicked politely and died, leaving Sam with his own heartbeat. He stood there so long that his father gently took the handle from him and hung it up.

The calls arrived nightly after that, punctual to the minute. "Ninety-nine." "Ninety-eight." "Ninety-seven." Nothing more was ever offered. His father called the phone company, who reported, with some discomfort, that no calls were being received at all. According to their records, the old line had been disconnected for eleven years. "A crossed wire," his father concluded, in the voice of a man convincing himself. Yet by December he had rearranged his evenings so that nine o'clock always found him in the kitchen. Sam checked the wall calendar every morning, as though the date might have moved in the night.

Sam approached the mystery the only way he knew: with a notebook and stubbornness. Every number was recorded with its date, and the pattern held without a single exception. One number less each night meant zero would arrive on the third of March. He marked the date in red and told nobody why. Without quite deciding to, he began spending his evenings watching the sky. The notebook filled slowly, one line a night, like a diary written by someone very far from home.

Winter tightened around the town while the numbers marched calmly downward. At school, Sam sat through lessons with the distracted face of a boy keeping a schedule nobody else could see. He considered telling his best friend and decided against it, twice. A secret shared is a secret spent, and this one still had work to do. At home, the ritual settled into the shape of family life. Dinner moved earlier, homework finished by half past eight, and the kitchen fell respectfully quiet before nine. Neither of them called it what it was, which is how families handle miracles.

In February, curiosity finally defeated caution. "Who are you?" Sam asked, the moment the line opened. "What are you counting?" The pause that followed was so long that he apologized into the silence. "I am very far away," the voice said at last, and there was warmth inside the tiredness. "Farther than your maps go. I have been traveling home for longer than you have been alive." "Old lines like yours are lighthouses. Yours is the last one still burning." "Keep answering, if you are willing. Every answer is a light. Forty-one."

From that night on, answering became a duty that Sam ranked above homework and sleep. He answered with a fever, once, and once by candlelight during a power cut that spared, mysteriously, the old line. The voice grew steadily nearer, losing its static the way a swimmer loses the sea. His father surrendered his theories one by one and took up making tea for the occasion instead. "Ten," said the voice one evening, and the kitchen felt like a station platform. The last three nights passed like held breath: three, two, one. It was the kind of waiting that makes a house feel like a held breath.

On the third of March, nine o'clock came and went in silence. Sam and his father stood shoulder to shoulder at the window, saying nothing. At ten past nine, a white light drew a slow, deliberate line across the sky. It was not falling; it was arriving, the difference somehow unmistakable. It settled beyond the hills without a sound, and the window glass sang one thin note. Then the ordinary night closed over the town like water over a stone.

Exactly one minute later, the old phone rang a single soft ring. They did not move, and they did not speak, and the tea went cold behind them. "I am home," the voice said, close and clear, like a neighbor across a fence. "Thank you for waiting. Thank you for every one of the fifty-nine answers." "You kept a light burning for a stranger. That is rarer than you know." The line closed gently, and the old phone has not made a sound since. The hills kept their usual shape, and the town went on selling bread and complaining about the weather. They never spoke of it to anyone, and nothing was ever reported about the hills. But the phone stays polished on the kitchen wall. And on clear nights Sam watches the sky with the patience of a lighthouse keeper. Somewhere out there, he is sure, another count has already begun for somebody else.

### C1
The black rotary phone had hung on the kitchen wall since before Sam's birth, a relic of a forgotten era that families sometimes preserve like an heirloom chair. Its weight felt like a small stone, and its polished surface reflected the dim morning light as if daring the room to forget its presence. Sam's father insisted that machines which still worked deserved to die on the wall rather than in a cardboard box, a sentiment he repeated to every curious visitor. When asked why he kept the antique, he would smile and answer, "Because it still works, and a working thing belongs on a wall." The copper wires behind the plaster were long dead, yet the phone's silence felt like a promise waiting for a call that might never come.

On a Tuesday in November, exactly nine o'clock, the phone rang, its shrill tone slicing the quiet kitchen like a sudden gasp. The sound startled Sam's father so much that his coffee cup slipped from his hand and shattered against the tiled floor. Sam lunged forward, grasped the cold, heavy receiver, and pressed the ear to his cheek, feeling the metal bite into his skin. Through a veil of static and distant hiss, a man's voice uttered a single word: "One hundred." The line clicked politely, then fell silent, leaving only Sam's own heartbeat echoing in the cramped room.

From that night onward, the phone rang precisely at nine o'clock each evening, each time delivering a number one less than the previous. "Ninety-nine," "Ninety-eight," "Ninety-seven," the voice recited, never offering any explanation beyond the descending tally. Sam's father called the telephone company, which, with evident discomfort, reported that the line had been disconnected for eleven years. Concluding that a crossed wire must be to blame, his father spoke in a tone that tried to convince both himself and Sam. Yet by December, Sam had rearranged his evenings so that nine o'clock found him standing in the kitchen, listening for the next inevitable number.

Determined to confront the mystery, Sam bought a plain notebook, opened it each night, and recorded the incoming digit beside the date. The sequence held without exception: each night the number fell by one, suggesting that when zero arrived, the count would cease on March third. He marked the anticipated end in red ink, a small act of rebellion against the invisible force that seemed to govern his days. Though he never told anyone, the notebook grew thicker, each line a solitary candle burning in the darkness of his thoughts. The habit became a ritual, a quiet promise to himself that he would not let the numbers fade unnoticed.

Winter settled over the town like a heavy blanket, and the numbers continued their steady descent, indifferent to the cold outside. At school, Sam's classmates noticed his distracted stare, but he concealed the secret behind a mask of ordinary teenage worries. He considered confiding in his best friend twice, yet each time he remembered the old adage that a shared secret loses its power. The house adjusted to the new rhythm: dinner arrived earlier, homework was finished by half past eight, and the kitchen fell hushed before nine. Neither parent named the phenomenon, for families often treat miracles as if they were ordinary, fragile things that might shatter if spoken aloud.

In February, curiosity finally eclipsed caution, and Sam lifted the receiver the moment the line clicked, demanding answers. "Who are you?" he asked, his voice trembling; "What are you counting?" followed, each question hanging in the static-filled air. The pause stretched longer than the ticking clock, prompting Sam to apologize to the empty silence. At last, a weary voice responded, "I am very far away, farther than your maps can reach, traveling home longer than you have lived." The voice added, "Old lines like yours are lighthouses; yours is the last one still burning. Keep answering, each reply is a light. Forty-one."

From that night onward, Sam treated each answer as a solemn duty, ranking it above homework and even sleep. He answered once during a thunderstorm, the room illuminated only by the flickering candle that refused to die. During a sudden power outage, the old phone rang again, its tone echoing strangely in the darkness, as if the line itself were spared. The voice grew clearer with each night, the static receding like tide pulling back from a shore. Sam's father, whose theories about crossed wires had crumbled, began preparing tea each evening, offering it as a quiet tribute to the unseen caller.

The countdown continued inexorably: ten, nine, eight, each number a step closer to an unknown destination. The final three nights arrived like held breaths, the house seeming to pause as the clock struck three, two, one. Sam stood beside his father at the kitchen window, eyes glued to the sky, feeling the weight of every unanswered second. The world outside seemed to hold its breath, the wind hushed, and even the distant traffic faded into a muted hum. When the clock finally struck midnight on March third, the line remained silent, as if the answer had already been given.

Ten minutes later, a thin white line scraped across the night sky, moving deliberately as though guided by an unseen hand. It was not a falling star but an arrival, its trajectory steady, settling beyond the distant hills without a sound. The glass of the kitchen window resonated with a single high-pitched note, as if the building itself acknowledged the event. The ordinary night then closed over the town like water over a stone, calm and indifferent to the marvel above. Sam felt a quiet reverence rise within him, the same feeling that a lighthouse keeper experiences watching a distant ship return.

Exactly one minute after the light vanished, the old phone emitted a single soft ring that seemed to echo through the stillness. Neither Sam nor his father moved; the tea grew cold, and the room held its breath as the voice returned. "I am home," the voice said, now close and clear, as if standing just beyond the kitchen door. "Thank you for waiting, for every one of the fifty-nine answers; you kept a light burning for a stranger, rarer than you know." The line clicked one final time, and the phone fell silent forever, its polished surface now a mute monument to the night.

The hills retained their familiar silhouettes, and the town continued its ordinary rhythm of bread sales and weather complaints. No one ever reported the strange light, and the phone remained on the wall, polished but untouched, a relic of an unseen pact. On clear nights, Sam steps onto the porch, eyes fixed on the horizon, waiting with the patience of a lighthouse keeper. He knows, deep down, that somewhere beyond the hills another count has already begun, waiting for a listener as diligent as himself. The old phone, though silent, continues to shine in his memory, a reminder that even forgotten things can still answer a call.

## st-0006 — The Whispering Well
Kaynak: ozgun

### A1
Ben moves to a very old house. It is far from the city. He finds a well under a cover. Ben takes the cover off. He looks down into the black water. He feels something bad. But he wants to know more.

Ben calls into the well. 'Who is there?' he asks. He wants his own sound back. But a low sound comes up. It says, 'I hear you.' Ben does not say that. He stands very still. He should put the cover back.

Next morning, Ben goes back. He asks another question. 'Who are you?' Ben asks. A low sound comes from the water. 'I wait under you,' it says. 'I am getting close.' Ben feels great fear. But he can not stop.

The next day, the sound changes. Ben asks, 'What do you want?' The sound is much higher now. It says, 'I want the light.' 'I do not see light for years.' Ben steps back. The thing is coming up. Ben should run.

Before night, Ben feels fear. He puts many things on the cover. A low sound comes from it. It says, 'You can not hold me.' 'I am near the top.' The sound is very near. Ben runs to his house. He closes the door.

At night, Ben can not close his eyes. He is in his black room. He hears a low sound on his floor. It says, 'I am outside your door.' Ben covers his face. He wants to leave now.

In the morning, there is light. Ben goes outside to see the well. The cover is in pieces. He sees water on the ground. The water goes to his door. Something is out of the well. It is now near his house.

Ben knows he must leave. He runs to his room. He takes his things and his car. Then, he hears a sound. Something is moving on the floor. The door starts to turn.

A low sound speaks his name. 'Ben,' it says. 'Open the door.' 'We can look at each other.' Ben does not open the door. He goes out of the window. He runs to his car. He drives away. Now, he lives in a city. But he still hears that sound. When there is water, he hears it.

### A2
Ben recently moved into a beautiful old house. It was near some dark trees. He spent his time in the large garden. One afternoon, he found a deep well. An old cover was over it. Ben removed the old cover. He looked inside. He saw black water at the bottom. Cold air came up. He felt fear, but he also had interest.

Ben wanted to test the deep well. He called, "I am here!" into the dark. He expected to hear his voice return. Instead, a quiet voice came from the bottom. It sounded like old paper on the ground. "I hear you," the voice said. Ben stood very still. Was his mind playing a game? He should have covered the well immediately.

The next morning, Ben went back to the garden. He wanted to hear the voice again. He put his hands on the cold side. "Who is down there?" he asked. His heart beat fast. A low sound came from the black water. Then, a quiet voice spoke. "I wait in the dark," the voice said. "I am getting stronger." Ben felt fear. He could not stop thinking about it.

The next day, the voice sounded different. It was closer to the top now. Ben asked, "What do you want?" The voice replied, "I want to feel the light." "I have not seen it for a hundred years." Ben took a step back. The thing was moving up. He should have run away.

That evening, Ben decided to close the well. He put heavy things on the old cover. Suddenly, a low laugh came from the cover. "These things cannot hold me," the voice said. "I am too close to the top." The voice was very near. Ben felt cold air on his face. He ran inside his house. He closed the door with his key.

Ben could not sleep that night. He lay in his dark bedroom. He listened to the trees outside. Suddenly, he heard a quiet voice from the floor. "I am just outside your door, Ben," it said. Ben covered his head in his bed. He wished he had never bought this house. He should have listened to his friends.

In the morning, Ben went to the garden. The heavy things were pushed away. The old cover was broken. A dark track of water led to the house. Something from the well was walking on the ground. Water marks stopped at his back door.

Ben knew he had to leave. He threw clothes into a box. He took his car keys. Then, he heard a sound from the other room. It was a slow, heavy pulling sound. The door began to move. A voice spoke his name. "Ben," it said.

"Open the door," the voice said. "We can look at each other in the light." Ben did not wait. He ran through the open window. He ran to his car. He drove away and never looked back. Now, Ben lives in a busy city. He always closes his doors with his key. Sometimes, in the dark, he hears voices from the walls.

### B1
Ben recently moved into a beautiful old house. It was near a quiet, dark forest far from the city. He spent most of his time in the large garden behind the property. One afternoon, he was clearing some thick plants. He discovered a deep stone well under an old wooden cover. He carefully removed the wet wood. He looked down into the black water at the bottom. A cold wind rose from the deep hole. This made him feel very nervous but also extremely curious.

Ben decided to shout a simple word into the darkness. "Hello!" he cried, expecting to hear his own voice back. Instead, a quiet whisper came from the bottom. It sounded like dry paper moving on the ground. "I hear you," the voice said. These were clearly not the words that Ben had spoken. He stood completely still in the quiet afternoon. He wondered if his mind was playing a trick on him. If he is careful, he will cover the well and walk away.

The next morning, Ben went back to the garden. He had a strong desire to hear the voice again. He leaned over the cold stone edge. "Who is down there?" he asked. His heart was beating very fast as he waited. A soft sound came from the black water. Then, a terrible whisper followed. "I am the one who waits in the dark," the voice said. "And I am getting warmer." The message made him afraid, but he could not stop thinking about it.

On the next day, the whisper sounded different. The voice did not seem to come from the very bottom anymore. Ben shouted, "What do you want?" The reply came from a position much higher up. "I want to feel the sun," the voice whispered. "I have not seen it for a hundred years." Ben took a step back in shock. He realized that the thing was climbing toward the light. If he stays here, he will be in great danger.

That evening, Ben decided that he must close the well. He gathered heavy stones from around the yard. He began to drop them onto the old wooden cover. Then, he heard a low laugh from between the pieces of wood. "Stones cannot hold me back," the voice whispered softly. "I am already too close to the top." The sound was so near that he felt its cold breath. He dropped his last stone and ran inside. He locked the door with shaking hands.

That night, Ben could not sleep at all. He lay in his dark bedroom, listening to the wind outside. Suddenly, he heard a familiar whisper from the floor. "I am just outside your door, Ben," the voice said. It sounded much clearer than before. He covered his ears with his sheet. He wished that he had never bought this old house. If he moves to another town, he will feel safe again.

In the morning, the bright sun made him feel a bit better. However, Ben knew that he was still not safe. He walked slowly into the garden to examine the well. To his shock, the heavy stones were pushed aside. The wooden cover was completely broken into pieces. A dark mark of wet ground led toward the back door. Something from the deep well was now walking outside. The wet marks stopped right at his kitchen door.

Ben realized that he had to leave immediately. He ran to his bedroom and threw some clothes into a bag. He grabbed his car keys from the table. As he prepared to run, he heard a noise. It was a slow, wet sound from the kitchen. He watched in fear as the door handle began to turn. A voice whispered his name from the other side of the wood.

"Ben," the voice whispered with a wet sound. "If you open the door, we can look at each other." "The light is beautiful today." Ben did not wait to see what was there. He jumped through the open living room window. He ran to his car and drove away without looking back. Now, he lives in a busy city. He always locks his doors and avoids wells. Sometimes, when it rains, he hears that wet whisper. It comes from the ground under his floor.

### B2
Ben had recently moved into a beautiful old house, which was situated near a quiet, dark forest far from the city. Although the house itself was wonderful, he spent most of his time exploring the large, wild garden behind the property. One afternoon, while he was clearing some thick plants, he discovered a deep stone well hidden under a wooden cover. If the cover had not been so old, it would have prevented him from looking inside the dark hole. He carefully removed the wet wood and looked down into the black water that lay silent at the very bottom. A cold wind seemed to rise from the depth, which made him feel quite nervous but also extremely curious.

To test the depth of the old well, Ben decided to shout a simple greeting down into the darkness. "Hello!" he cried, expecting to hear his own voice returned to him after a brief moment of silence. Instead of a normal echo, a quiet whisper arose from the bottom, sounding like dry paper moving on the ground. "I hear you," the voice said, which was clearly not the word that Ben had spoken into the well. He stood completely still, wondering if his mind was playing a trick on him during this silent afternoon. If he had been wiser, he would have covered the well immediately and walked back to his house.

The next morning, Ben was drawn back to the garden by an intense desire to hear the voice again. He leaned over the stone edge, which was cold against his hands, and called out another question. "Who is down there?" he asked, his heart beating fast as he waited for the strange response. A soft sigh came from the black water, followed by a message that was whispered with a terrible tone. "I am the one who waits in the dark, and I am getting warmer," the voice replied. Although the message frightened him, he could not stop thinking about the unknown person who lived under the earth.

On the next day, Ben noticed a significant change in the sound of the whispers coming from the well. When he shouted, "What do you want?" the reply did not seem to come from the very bottom anymore. Instead, the words were whispered from a position that was several meters higher than the cold, dark water. "I want to feel the sun, which I have not seen for a hundred years," the voice whispered back. Ben took a step back, realizing that whatever was inside the well was actively climbing toward the light. If he had possessed any sense of safety, he would have run away and never returned to that garden.

That evening, filled with fear, Ben decided that the well must be closed to protect himself from danger. He gathered several heavy stones from around the yard and began to drop them onto the old wooden cover. As each stone was placed, a low laugh was heard from the narrow gaps between the rough pieces of wood. "Stones cannot hold me back, because I am already too close to the top," the voice whispered softly. The sound was so near that Ben could almost feel the cold breath of the speaker on his face. He dropped his last stone and ran inside his house, locking the door behind him with shaking hands.

That night, Ben could not sleep, as his mind was filled with images of the thing in the well. He lay in his dark bedroom, listening to the wind that blew through the trees outside his window. Suddenly, a sound arose that was not the wind; it was a familiar whisper coming from the floor. "I am just outside your door, Ben," the voice said, sounding much clearer than it had before. He covered his ears with his sheet, wishing that he had never bought this terrible, old country house. If he had listened to the warnings of the neighbors, he would have moved to a different town.

When morning finally came, the bright sun provided some comfort, but Ben knew he was not safe. He walked slowly into the garden to examine the well, expecting to find the heavy stones still in place. To his shock, the stones had been pushed aside, and the wooden cover was completely broken into pieces. A dark trail of wet ground led from the open hole toward the back door of his house. It was clear that whatever had been living in the deep well was now walking on the grass. If he had looked closer, he would have seen the wet marks that stopped right at his kitchen door.

Ben realized that he had to leave immediately, before the sun went down and darkness returned to the valley. He ran to his bedroom, threw some clothes into a bag, and grabbed his car keys from the table. As he was preparing to run out the front door, he heard a soft sound coming from the kitchen. It was the slow, wet sound of something heavy dragging itself across the wooden floor toward the hall. Then, he watched in fear as the handle of the kitchen door began to turn slowly. A voice, which was now extremely close, whispered his name from the other side of the wood.

"Ben," the voice whispered, sounding like a wet throat trying to speak after a long period of silence. "If you opened the door, we could finally look at each other in the beautiful light." Ben did not wait to see what was behind the door; he jumped through the open living room window instead. He ran to his car, started the engine, and drove away from the house without looking back even once. Now, he lives in a busy city, but he always locks his doors and avoids any place with a well. Sometimes, when it rains, he still hears that wet whisper coming from the pipes under his floor.

### C1
Ben had just settled into a grand, weather-worn manor that perched on the edge of a silent, shadowy forest far from the city's bustle. The house, with its cracked plaster and ivy-clad towers, exuded a melancholy beauty that both intrigued and unnerved him. Every afternoon he wandered the overgrown garden, where tangled hedges and wild grasses seemed to guard secret paths. The garden stretched far behind the house, a tangled maze of ancient oaks and forgotten stone statues. Ben felt a strange pull toward the deepest, most concealed corners, as if the land itself whispered his name.

One humid afternoon, while hacking away at a thicket of brambles, his shovel struck a cold, rounded stone hidden beneath a rotting wooden lid. The lid, swollen with damp and time, had warped into a seamless disc that concealed the darkness below. He lifted the soggy timber, and a faint, stale breath seemed to rise from the abyss, chilling his skin. The well yawned like a black mouth, its stone walls slick with moss and the faint echo of distant water. A sudden gust, as if summoned from the depths, brushed his face, filling him with both dread and an irresistible curiosity.

Ben crouched at the rim, his fingertips tingling against the cold stone, and peered into the inky void that swallowed any hint of light. He imagined the well as a portal to another world, a silent witness to centuries of forgotten prayers. A thin ribbon of wind curled upward, carrying with it the faint scent of damp earth and distant pine. The sound of his own breathing seemed amplified, as though the well itself were listening. A shiver ran down his spine, half warning, half invitation, and he could not resist testing the darkness.

Summoning courage, Ben shouted a simple greeting into the abyss, his voice echoing off the stone like a thrown stone. He waited, ears straining for the familiar bounce of his own words, but the silence that follows felt heavier than any echo. Instead, a faint rustle rose from the bottom, resembling dry paper sliding across a wooden floor. "I hear you," whispered a voice that was neither his own nor any sound he could name. Ben froze, heart thudding against his ribs, wondering whether his mind had conjured the phantom reply.

The following morning, an inexplicable pull drew him back to the well before sunrise had even painted the sky. He pressed his cold palms against the stone rim, feeling the chill seep into his bones. "Who is down there?" he called, his voice trembling like a candle in a draft. A soft sigh rose from the black water, followed by a voice tinged with a terrible, ancient weariness. "I am the one who waits in the dark, and I am getting warmer," it murmured, sending a cold shiver through the garden.

On the third day, curiosity had become a gnawing obsession, and Ben approached the well with a fierce determination. He shouted, "What do you want?" and waited as the darkness seemed to pulse in answer. The reply did not echo from the abyssal bottom but rose from a point several meters above the stagnant water. "I want to feel the sun, which I have not seen for a hundred years," the voice whispered, trembling with longing. A sudden dread washed over Ben as he realized the entity was climbing toward the surface, inch by inch.

That evening, trembling hands gathered heavy stones from the garden, each slab slick with moss and the scent of rain. He lifted the stones one by one, placing them atop the rotting cover as if building a barrier against the unseen. Each impact produced a low, resonant thud, and between the cracks a faint, mocking laugh slipped out. "Stones cannot hold me back, because I am already too close to the top," the voice hissed, breath cold on his ear. A shiver ran along his spine as the whisper brushed his cheek, and he scrambled inside, slamming the door behind him.

Night settled like a thick blanket, and Ben lay in his dim bedroom, the wind sighing through the trees outside. His thoughts swirled like autumn leaves, each one a memory of the well's echoing promise. Suddenly, a sound rose that was not the wind; a familiar whisper slipped from the floorboards. "I am just outside your door, Ben," it breathed, clearer than any previous murmur, as if standing beside him. He clutched the sheet, his heart hammering, wishing he had heeded the warnings of the village folk.

When dawn finally broke, its pale light spilled over the garden, offering a brief respite from the night's terror. Ben hurried to the well, expecting the stones he had piled to still lie in place. To his horror, the stones lay scattered, the wooden cover shattered into splinters as if smashed by invisible fists. A dark, wet trail of earth stretched from the open mouth of the well toward the back door of the house. Ben realized, with a sudden, icy clarity, that whatever lingered within the well had already begun its climb onto the grass.

Panic surged; he flung a bag of clothes onto a chair, snatched his car keys, and raced toward the front door. From the kitchen, a wet, dragging sound crept across the floorboards, slow and deliberate, as if something heavy were being pulled. He turned, heart pounding, to see the kitchen door's handle turning slowly, inch by inch, under an unseen force. A voice, now close enough to brush his cheek, whispered his name with a wet, guttural tone. "Ben," it hissed, "if you open the door, we shall finally meet in the beautiful light." The terror forced him to leap through the living-room window.

He scrambled onto the porch, slammed the car door, and drove away without glancing back, the house receding like a nightmare. Now he lives in a bustling city, where the clamor of traffic masks any whisper of wind. He double-locks every door, avoids any place with an old well, and feels a lingering chill whenever rain taps his windows. Sometimes, on stormy nights, a wet murmur seeps through the pipes beneath his floor, reminding him of that dark water. He never forgets the lesson that some secrets are better left buried, lest they rise again to whisper your name.

## st-0007 — The Mirror's Shadow
Kaynak: ozgun

### A1
Leo moves into a large house. It is near a very old town. He visits a small shop. He sees a very old, black mirror. He buys it for his room. He puts it near his bed. At first, the mirror is good.

During the first night, Leo sits up. The room is very different now. He looks into the mirror. He sees a black figure. The figure sits near the door. There is no figure in his room.

On the second night, he sits up. He looks at the mirror again. The black figure is standing now. It takes one step forward. The room has no sound. Leo feels very bad.

On the third night, it moves closer. Leo wants to take the mirror down. He tries to move it. But a strong force holds it. He puts a white cover on it.

On the fourth night, he sits up. The white cover is down. The black figure is very close. It has no clear face. It looks at him. His body cannot move.

In the morning, Leo reads old books. They are about his house. A young man leaves this room. He always looks at the mirror. He never returns. Leo has a big problem.

On the fifth night, Leo watches the room. He keeps the lights on. Then, every light goes out. The room is very black. The black figure is very near. He hears a sound. It calls his name.

On the sixth night, the figure is close. Its hands are on the mirror. The mirror moves like water. A black hand comes out. It holds his face. It pulls Leo into the mirror.

The next day, the house has no one. People cannot find Leo. The mirror goes to a local shop. Another customer buys the mirror. She takes it to her room. A black figure is already waiting.

### A2
Leo moved into a large, quiet house. It was near an old town. He wanted to buy some nice things. He visited a small shop. He saw a large mirror. The mirror was very dark, old, and special. He bought it immediately. He put it on the wall near his bed. That evening, the glass showed his clean room. He saw his happy face in it.

During the first night, Leo suddenly opened his eyes. The air in his room was very cold. He sat up and looked at the large mirror. He saw a dark shadow in the mirror. It sat on a seat near the window. But there was no seat in his real room. The shadow did not move. Leo felt a deep worry in his heart. He thought it was only a shadow from the trees.

On the second night, Leo watched the mirror. He was awake late in the night. He looked at the mirror immediately. The shadow was still there. But it was not on the seat now. The dark figure was standing. It took one step forward. There was no sound in the room. The room was completely quiet. This made Leo feel great fear.

On the third night, the shadow took another step. It was closer to his bed. Leo decided to take the mirror off the wall. He tried to pull it down. But a strong force held it there. It did not move at all. He could not run away from this bad dream. He covered the glass with a white cover. He hoped he would not see it again.

On the fourth night, the cover was on the floor. It was clean and white. He looked into the mirror. The shadow took two more steps. It stood in the middle of the mirror. The dark figure had no face. It was looking directly at him. Leo wanted to run away. But his body was not able to move. He could only watch the bad shadow.

The next morning, Leo wanted to find some information. He reads old books about his house. A young man lived in his room many years ago. The young man was suddenly gone. Reports said he always looked at the dark mirror. Leo realized the same thing was happening to him. He felt a great fear.

On the fifth night, Leo stayed awake. He kept all his bedroom lights on. Suddenly, every light went out. The room was very dark. But the mirror had its own cold light. The shadow stood at the side. It was ready to step out. Leo heard a quiet voice from the glass. The voice called his name again and again.

On the sixth night, Leo could not avoid the shadow. The dark figure reached the front of the glass. The glass began to move like water. A cold, dark hand came out of the mirror. It reached toward Leo. He could not move because of his fear. The cold hand touched him. He was pulled slowly into the dark mirror.

The next day, no one was in the house. The police could not find Leo. The mirror was still on the wall. Later, his things were sold to a local shop. Another customer bought the large mirror. She took it to her home. She put it in her bedroom. Inside the glass, a dark shadow was already waiting.

### B1
Leo recently moved into a large, quiet house near an old town. He wanted some special furniture, so he visited a small shop. A large mirror with a dark wood frame caught his eye. It looked very ancient and special. If he learns about its strange history, he will choose a different object. But he bought it and hung it on his bedroom wall. It was directly opposite his comfortable bed. That first evening, the glass reflected his room perfectly. It showed only the clean floor and his happy face.

During the first night, Leo woke up suddenly. The air in his bedroom was very cold. He sat up and looked at the large mirror. In the glass, he saw a dark shadow. It was sitting on a wood chair near the window. However, when he looked at the real room, there was no chair. The shadow in the mirror did not move. Its presence made him feel a deep worry. He tried to believe it was just a strange effect. Perhaps the trees outside his window caused it.

On the second night, Leo watched the mirror carefully. Then he turned off his light. When he woke up, he immediately looked at the glass. The shadow was still there. But it was not sitting on the chair anymore. Instead, the dark figure was now standing. It had taken one clear step forward. If the shadow is a real person, Leo will hear its steps. But the bedroom was completely silent. This made the situation seem very real and terrible.

By the third night, the shadow had taken another step. It was closer to his bed. Leo decided he must remove the mirror. He believed this action would solve his terrible problem. He tried to pull the heavy frame off the wall. But a strange force held it there. He pulled hard, but the mirror did not move. It seemed like part of the house. He felt a sudden panic. He realized he was unable to escape. He covered the glass with a thick white sheet. He hoped he would not see it again.

However, during the fourth night, the sheet was on the floor. It was completely dry and clean. It seemed as if hands had removed the cover while he slept. He looked into the mirror. The shadow had taken two more steps. It stood right in the middle. The dark figure had no clear face. It was looking directly at him. Leo wanted to run, but his legs felt very heavy. They felt like stone. He could only lie there. He watched the shadow grow larger and more dangerous.

The next morning, Leo visited the local library. He wanted information about the house and its previous owners. He reads several old books and records. He wanted to discover the origin of the mirror. He learned that a young man lived there many years ago. The young man had disappeared without a trace. Reports said he spent his last days looking into the mirror. If a person stays here too long, they will disappear. This filled Leo with a deep fear. He realized the same thing was happening to him.

On the fifth night, Leo decided to stay awake. He kept all his lights turned on. However, late at night, every light in the house went out. The room was in complete darkness. But the mirror had its own cold light. The shadow was standing at the very edge. It seemed ready to step out of the glass. Leo heard a quiet, cold whisper from the other side. The whisper called his name over and over. The voice was quiet and very ancient.

On the sixth night, Leo has run out of time. He cannot avoid the shadow. The dark figure has reached the front of the mirror. Its hands are pressed against the glass. Suddenly, the glass does not look solid. It begins to move like water. A cold, dark hand slowly comes out of the mirror. It reaches toward Leo, who is frozen with fear. If he does not run now, he will not survive. The cold fingers touch his skin. He feels himself being pulled into the dark glass.

The next day, the house was completely empty. The local police found no trace of Leo. His bed was empty. The beautiful mirror was still hanging on the wall. A few weeks later, his furniture was sold to a local shop. Another customer bought the large mirror. She had just moved into a new apartment nearby. If she learns what is waiting inside, she will leave it behind. But she smiled, took it home, and hung it up. In her bedroom, a dark shadow was already waiting.

### B2
Leo had recently moved into a large, quiet house that was situated near the edge of an old, historic town. He wanted to find some unique furniture, so he visited a small shop where various beautiful things were sold cheap. A large mirror with a dark wooden frame immediately caught his eye because it looked extremely ancient and special. If he had known about the mirror's strange history, he probably would have chosen a different object for his bedroom. However, he bought it without delay and hung it on the wall directly opposite his comfortable new bed. That first evening, the glass reflected his room perfectly, showing nothing but the clean floor and his own happy face.

During the first night, Leo woke up suddenly because the air in his bedroom had become surprisingly cold and thin. He sat up in bed, opened his eyes, and looked at the large mirror which was shining under the moon. In the reflection, he noticed a dark shadow sitting quietly on a wooden chair that was placed near the window. However, when he turned his head to look at the actual room, he realized that no such chair existed there. The shadow in the mirror did not move, but its presence made him feel an intense sense of deep worry. He tried to convince himself that it was merely a strange effect caused by the trees outside his window.

On the second night, Leo watched the mirror carefully before he decided to turn off the light near his bed. When he woke up during the night, he immediately looked at the glass to see if the figure was still there. The shadow was indeed there, but it was no longer sitting on the silent chair near the far wall. Instead, the dark figure was now standing, having taken one clear step forward toward the center of the reflected room. If the shadow had been a real person, Leo would have heard the sound of steps on the wooden floor. But the bedroom remained completely silent, which only made the growing situation seem much more frightening and real to him.

By the third night, the shadow had taken another step, moving closer to the bed where Leo lay awake. He decided that he must remove the mirror from his bedroom, believing that this action would solve his terrible problem. He tried to pull the heavy frame off the wall, but it was held by some strange, unknown force. No matter how hard he pulled, the mirror would not move, as if it had become part of the house. He felt a sudden panic rising in his chest, realizing that he was completely unable to escape this bad dream. He covered the glass with a thick white sheet, hoping that he would not have to look at it again.

However, when he woke up during the fourth night, the sheet was lying on the floor, completely dry and clean. It was as if some hands had gently removed the cover while he was sleeping deeply in his bed. He looked into the mirror and saw that the shadow had taken two more steps, standing right in the middle. The figure, which was dark and had no clear face, was now staring directly at him through the glass. Leo wanted to run out of the house, but his legs felt extremely heavy, as if they were made of stone. He could only lie there, watching the shadow that seemed to grow larger and more dangerous with every passing minute.

The next morning, Leo visited the local library to find some information about the old house and its previous owners. He read through several old books and records, hoping to discover the origin of the terrible mirror he had bought. He discovered that a young man had once lived in his bedroom many decades ago, before disappearing without a trace. According to the reports, the young man had spent his last days staring into the same large, dark mirror. If the young man had destroyed the mirror, he would probably have survived his terrible experience and remained alive. This discovery filled Leo with a deep fear, as he realized that the same thing was currently happening to him.

When the fifth night arrived, Leo decided to stay awake, keeping all the lights in his bedroom turned on. However, late at night, every single light in the house suddenly went out, leaving his room in complete and heavy darkness. In the mirror, which seemed to have its own cold light, the shadow was standing at the very edge. It was so close that it seemed ready to step out of the glass and enter his real room. Leo could hear a quiet, cold whisper that seemed to come directly from the other side of the glass. The whisper was calling his name, over and over, with a voice that was both quiet and extremely ancient.

On the sixth night, Leo knew that he had run out of time and could no longer avoid the shadow. The dark figure in the mirror had finally reached the absolute front, its hands pressed against the glass surface. Suddenly, the glass did not look solid anymore; instead, it began to move like water under a gentle wind. A cold, dark hand slowly emerged from the mirror, reaching out toward Leo who was frozen with extreme fear. If he had run away on the first day, he would have been safe from this terrible house. But now, the cold fingers touched his skin, and he felt himself being pulled slowly into the dark reflection.

The next day, the house was completely empty, and the local police could find no trace of the young man. His bed was completely empty, and the beautiful mirror was still hanging on the wall, shining in the morning light. A few weeks later, all of Leo's furniture was sold to a local shop that dealt in old, interesting items. Another customer, who had just moved into a new apartment nearby, bought the large mirror with the wooden frame. If she knew what was waiting inside the glass, she would certainly have left the mirror in the shop. But she smiled, took it home, and hung it in her bedroom, where a dark shadow was already waiting.

### C1
Leo had recently taken possession of a sprawling, silent house that perched on the fringe of an ancient, cobblestone town. The rooms were bathed in pale morning light, their high ceilings echoing the faint creak of settling timber. Seeking a piece of character for his new bedroom, Leo wandered into a cramped antique shop where dust seemed to settle like a veil. Among the assorted bric-a-brac, a tall mirror framed in dark, gnawed wood seized his attention, its surface blackened by age and mystery. He bought it without hesitation, unaware that the object's hidden past would soon eclipse the comfort of his freshly made bed.

Back at the house, he fixed the mirror to the wall directly opposite his newly arranged bed, the frame fitting the stone as if it had always belonged there. That night, the moonlit light slipped through the narrow window, casting a silver sheen across the glass that reflected his tidy room with uncanny precision. He smiled at his own relaxed expression, feeling a fleeting sense of triumph at having found such a unique ornament for his sanctuary. The house settled around him, the old floorboards sighing in rhythm with his breathing, and a gentle chill brushed his skin as sleep claimed him. Unaware of the darkness gathering behind the glass, Leo drifted into dreams, his thoughts unburdened by the unseen presence that would soon awaken.

At the stroke of midnight a sudden cold seeped into the bedroom, thin as a whisper, jolting Leo from slumber. He sat up, heart hammering, and the moon-lit mirror glimmered with an eerie glow that seemed to pulse with a life of its own. In the reflection, a dark silhouette perched silently upon a wooden chair placed near the window, its outline stark against the pale floor. When he turned his head to scan the actual room, his eyes fell on empty space; no chair stood there, only the bare wall. A wave of unease surged through him, yet he tried to rationalize the image as a trick of moonlight and trembling trees.

The following evening, Leo kept the bedroom lamp dim, his eyes trained on the glass as he prepared to sleep. When the darkness pressed against his eyelids, he glanced at the mirror, half-expecting the phantom chair to have vanished. The shadow was still there, but now it stood, having taken a single, deliberate step toward the centre of the reflected room. If a living being had occupied the space, the floor would have announced its presence with a soft creak, yet the silence remained absolute. The realization that the image moved independently of any physical cause tightened a knot of terror in his chest.

On the third night the darkness seemed to thicken, a viscous blanket that muffled even the faint ticking of the hallway clock. The shadow had taken another step, now inching toward the bed where Leo lay, its outline growing sharper with each breath he drew. Determined to end the nightmare, he grasped the mirror's frame and pulled, but an unseen strength held it fast as if the house itself resisted. His muscles strained, his heart hammered, yet the glass would not budge, its surface merging with the plaster as though it were a living part of the wall. In desperation he draped a thick white sheet over the mirror, hoping the darkness behind it would be sealed away from his eyes.

When the fourth night arrived, Leo awoke to find the sheet crumpled on the floor, dry and untouched, as though an invisible hand had lifted it. He stared into the glass and saw the shadow two steps further, now standing in the centre of the reflected room, its form opaque and featureless. Its stare met his, a hollow gaze that seemed to pierce through the barrier of glass, demanding his attention with an ancient, wordless accusation. Leo's legs felt as though they were forged from stone, each movement a laborious effort, and he could not summon the strength to flee. He lay there, heart pounding, watching the dark figure swell with each passing second, its presence growing more oppressive than the night itself.

At dawn, Leo walked to the town's modest library, its stone facade echoing the same quiet melancholy that haunted his bedroom. He pored over yellowed ledgers and brittle newspapers, searching for any mention of the house or its former residents. Among the dust-covered pages he uncovered a terse report about a young man who had vanished in the very bedroom Leo now occupied. The article whispered that the youth had spent his final days staring into a large, dark-framed mirror, his fate sealed by his own curiosity. A chill ran down Leo's spine as he imagined the mirror's relentless pull, realizing that he might be reenacting the same doomed pattern.

On the fifth night Leo resolved to stay awake, flooding the room with bright lamps, the harsh light stabbing at the darkness like knives. Midnight arrived, and without warning every bulb in the house sputtered out, plunging the interior into an oppressive, absolute black. The mirror, however, emitted its own cold luminescence, revealing the shadow perched at the very edge of the glass, poised to cross. A thin, icy whisper slipped from the surface, chanting Leo's name in a voice that seemed older than the house itself. The syllables repeated, each echo a reminder that the barrier between worlds was thinning, and that he was no longer merely an observer.

On the sixth night, exhaustion gave way to dread as the dark figure finally arrived at the very front of the mirror, its palms pressed flat against the surface. The glass, no longer a solid barrier, rippled like water disturbed by a gentle wind, shimmering with a sickly, blue-gray sheen. From the wavering pane, a cold, skeletal hand emerged, its fingers elongated and trembling, reaching toward Leo with a desperate, inexorable pull. He felt the icy contact brush his skin, a sensation that froze his blood and anchored his body as though shackled by unseen chains. In that instant, the realization struck him that the choice to remain had sealed his fate, and the mirror claimed another soul.

When dawn broke, the house stood silent and empty, the bed vacant, the mirror still hanging, its frame glinting in the pale morning light. Police officers combed the rooms, finding no trace of Leo or the vanished young man, only the lingering chill that clung to the walls. The mirror, untouched, seemed to watch the investigators with an indifferent gleam, as if bored by their futile curiosity. A few weeks later, the estate's contents were auctioned to a shop that specialized in antiquities, and the mirror was displayed among other curiosities. A new tenant, a young woman moving into a nearby apartment, purchased the dark-framed piece, smiling at its age-worn elegance, unaware of the waiting darkness.

She hung the mirror opposite her own bed, arranging the room with care, and as she stepped back, a familiar chill brushed her neck. In the reflected surface, a dark silhouette already waited, its featureless head turned toward her, eyes unseen yet somehow accusing. She whispered a nervous laugh, but the mirror's cold glow answered with a silent promise that the cycle would continue. The house beyond the street kept its secrets, and the ancient shadow prepared once more to step out of the glass and into another life. And so the mirror waited, patient as the night, for the next curious soul to glance into its depths and awaken the darkness within.
