## st-0008 — The Happy Prince, and Other Tales
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
The Good Prince figure is high. People look at him. A mother tells child, "Be like Prince." He never cries. A man says, "He feels good." No money children leave a building. They say, "He looks like a person." Their hard teacher does not like bad ideas.

One night, a small Swallow goes. He goes over the city. His friends go to Egypt. He stays for a Reed by the water. He meets her early. He spends warm days with her. Other Swallows say, "Love is not good." She has no money. She has much family.

Then not warm starts. Other Swallows go away. He feels by himself. "I love to go." "My friend must come." Reed does not go. He calls, "I go to Pyramids!"

He goes all day. Night, he is in city. "Where will I stay?" he asks. He sees the high figure. "I will stay here," he says. "It is a good place." He lands near Prince's base. He has a room for sleep.

He puts his head down. A large bit of water falls. He says, "Different!" No moving here. Lights are clear. But water falls. Another bit falls. He says, "Figure does not help." He wants to go away. One more bit falls.

He looks up. He sees the Prince's eyes. They are full of water. Water on his face. He asks, "Who are you?" "Why do you cry?" "I am the Good Prince." "I live, I am good." I see city problem now. So I must cry.

The Swallow thinks, "Not all money?" The Prince speaks. In a small way, a house is there. Woman makes things. She makes green things for a special person. Hands feel bad from work. Little child is bad. He asks for food. She has only water.

"Swallow," says the Prince. "Will you bring my thing?" "It is from my side." "My base is here. I cannot move." The Swallow answers. My friends go over the Nile. They sleep in a King's last place. King has a thing.

"Swallow," says the Prince. "Will you stay one night?" "Will you be my helper?" Child needs water. Mother feels bad. Swallow says, "I do not like children." "Last warm days, bad children." They send small things. They never come near me. "Swallows go very well."

### A2
The Happy Prince figure was high above the city. People saw him. Said he was beautiful. A mother told her kid. "Be like the Happy Prince." He makes no water. A not happy man said. "I am happy. One is happy." Children left the church. They said, "He looks like a good spirit." Their teacher was serious. He did not like their dreams.

One night, a small Swallow flew above the city. His friends left for Egypt weeks ago. But he stayed here. He loved a Reed by the water line. She was beautiful. He met her in warm season. He liked her small body. He stayed with her all summer. Other Swallows said, "This is a wrong love." She had many family members.

Then cold season came. Other Swallows flew away. He felt alone. He did not like the Reed. "She does not talk," he said. "She plays wind." He told her, "I love travel." "My wife must love it." He asked, "Will you come?" But the Reed said no. He made water. "You played with my mind!" "I am going to Pyramids!"

He flew all day. At night, he came to the city. "Where will I stay?" he asked. He saw the beautiful figure on a high support. "I will stay there!" he made water. The air is clean. He landed between the feet of the Happy Prince. He said, "My room is yellow metal." He was ready to sleep.

He put his head to rest. A large drop of water fell. It fell on him. "How not normal!" he made water. "No air water is here." Stars give much light. But water is falling. Another drop fell. He said, "What use is a figure?" "It cannot stop water fall." He wanted to fly away. A number three drop fell.

He looked up. He saw the Prince making water. Water ran down his yellow metal face. The Swallow felt not happy for him. "Who are you?" he asked. "Why are you making water?" "You made me full of water." "I am the Happy Prince," he said. "When not dead, I was not happy." "I lived in Sans-Souci Palace." "Now I am dead." "I am high." "I see city problems." "I must make water."

The Swallow thought, "He is not all yellow metal!" "Far away," the Prince said. His voice was good. "In a small street, a poor woman makes clothes." "She puts plant beauty on a dress." It is for the Queen's worker. Her face is small, needs rest. Her red hands hurt. Her hard work hurt them. Her little boy is not well. He has high body heat, asks for plant food. She has only water line water for him.

"Swallow, Swallow, little Swallow," said the Prince. "Bring her the red rock." "My feet cannot move." "I cannot move." The Swallow answered. "My friends fly over the Nile water." "Soon they will sleep." "They will be in the dead King place." "The King is in his many colors box." "He has light color cover part on him." "Around his head are light green rocks."

"Swallow, Swallow, little Swallow," said the Prince. "Stay with me one night." "Be my helper?" "The boy wants water much." "His mother is very not happy." The Swallow said, "I do not like boys." "Last summer, two bad boys threw rocks." "They threw them at me by the water line." "They did not hit me." "Swallows fly very well."

### B1
The Happy Prince figure stood high above the city. People often looked at him. They spoke about his beauty. A smart mother told her crying child. She said, "Be like the Happy Prince." "He never cries." A man was not happy. He said, "I am glad." "Someone in the world is quite happy." Good Children left the church. They said, "He looks like a good spirit." Their teacher looked serious. He did not approve of children dreaming.

One night, a small Swallow flew over the city. His friends had gone to Egypt. They left six weeks before. But he had stayed behind. He loved a beautiful Reed by the river. This was the reason he stayed. He met her early in the spring. Her thin body attracted him. He spent the whole summer with her. Other Swallows talked about his love. They said, "This is a strange love." "She has no money." "She has too many family members."

Then fall came. All the other Swallows flew away. He felt alone. He began to get tired of the Reed. He loved her. But he thought, "She has no conversation." He also thought, "She plays with the wind." He told her, "I love to travel." "My wife should also love it." Finally, he asked, "Will you come with me?" But the Reed shook her head. So he cried with a loud voice. "You have been playing with my heart!" "I am going to the Pyramids!" "I must go!"

All day long he flew. At night, he arrived at the city. "Where shall I stay?" he asked. He hoped the town was ready. Then he saw the beautiful figure. It was on a tall column. "I will stay there," he cried. "It is a fine position." "It has fresh air." So he landed between the feet of the Happy Prince. He said, "I have a bright bedroom." Then he prepared to sleep.

He put his head under his bird part. Then a large drop of water fell on him. "What a strange thing!" he cried. "There is not a single dark air in the sky." "The stars are quite clear and bright." "Yet it is raining." Then another drop fell on him. He said, "What is the use of a figure?" "If it cannot keep the rain off, what is its use?" He decided to fly away. But a next drop fell.

He looked up. He saw the Happy Prince's eyes. They were filled with tears. Tears ran down his bright face sides. The Swallow felt much sad care. "Who are you?" he asked. "Why are you crying?" "You have made me very full of water." "I am the Happy Prince," the figure answered. "When I was alive, I did not know pain." "I lived in Sans-Souci Palace." "Now I am dead and high above the city." "I see all my city's great pain." "I must cry."

The Swallow thought, "What!" "Is he not all gold?" He was too good mannered to speak this. "Far away," the Prince continued. He spoke in a soft, nice voice. "In a little street, there is a poor house." A woman makes clothes there. She is making beautiful flowers. They are for a dress. The dress is for the Queen's helper. Her face is thin and tired. Her red hands are hurt. A sharp tool hurts them from hard work. Her little boy is sick. He has a high temperature. He is asking for fruit. But she has only river water. She can give him only water.

"Swallow, Swallow, little Swallow," said the Prince. "Will you not bring her the red stone?" "It is from my metal weapon handle." "My feet are stuck to this base." "I cannot move from here." The Swallow answered him. "My friends are flying over the Nile river." "Soon they will sleep." "They will sleep in the great King's resting place." "The King's body is there." "The King is in his bright box for the dead." "He is covered in yellow fabric." "Around his neck is a neck chain." "It is a neck chain of light green stone."

"Swallow, Swallow, little Swallow," said the Prince. "Will you not stay with me?" "Stay for one night." "Will you be my helper?" "The boy needs a drink." "His mother is so sad." The Swallow said, "I do not think I like boys." "Last summer, two bad boys threw stones at me." "I was by the river." "They never hit me, of course." "Swallows fly too well for that."

### B2
The Happy Prince figure stood high above the city. This important figure was placed where many people could see it. People often looked up at him. They spoke about his great beauty. A wise mother told her crying child, "You should be like the Happy Prince. He never cries." A man who was not happy also said, "I am glad someone in the world is quite happy." Charity children were leaving the church. They said, "He looks just like a good spirit." Their teacher looked very serious. He did not approve of children dreaming, so he did not like their talk.

One night, a small Swallow flew over the city. He had flown a long way. His friends had gone to Egypt six weeks before this night. But he had stayed behind them. This was because he loved a beautiful Reed. She lived by the river. He met her early in the spring season. He was attracted by her thin body. He spent the whole summer with her near the water. Other Swallows talked about his choice. They said, "This is a silly love for you. She has no money and too many family members." They thought this was a bad idea for him.

Then autumn came to the land. All the other Swallows flew away to warmer places. He felt quite alone. He began to get tired of the Reed he loved so much. He often thought about her. "She has no conversation," he said to himself. "And she often plays with the wind." He told the Reed, "I love to travel to new places. My wife should also love to travel." If she loved travel, his life would be better. Finally, he asked her a question. "Will you come away with me?" The Reed shook her head. He cried, "You play with my heart!" He then said, "I must go to the Pyramids. Good-bye!"

All day long he flew across the land. At night, he arrived at the city. He had flown very far. He asked himself, "Where shall I stay tonight?" He was hoping the town would be ready for him. Then he saw the beautiful figure. It stood on a tall column in the city center. "I will stay there," he cried with joy. "It is a fine position with fresh air." He thought it would be a good place. So he landed just between the feet of the Happy Prince figure. He said to himself, "I have a golden bedroom here." He then prepared to sleep for the night.

Just as he put his head under his wing, a large drop of water fell on him. He felt the cold water. "What a strange thing!" he cried out loud. "There is not a single cloud in the sky. The stars are quite clear and bright. Yet, it is raining down here." He was very confused by this. Then another drop fell on him. He said, "What use is a figure if it cannot keep the rain off?" He was not happy about this. He decided to fly away from the wet. But another drop fell on him.

He looked up toward the figure. He saw the Happy Prince's eyes. They were filled with tears. Large tears ran down his golden cheeks. The Swallow felt much regret for him. He asked, "Who are you, great figure?" He also asked, "Why are you crying? You have made me very wet." The figure answered, "I am the Happy Prince." He continued, "When I was alive, I lived in Sans-Souci Palace. I did not know any unhappiness then." He paused for a moment. "Now, I am dead and placed high above the town. I see all my city's pain. I must cry because of this."

The Swallow thought to himself, "What! Is he not all gold, as he seems?" This thought was not said out loud. The Swallow was too well-behaved to speak this thought. The Prince continued to speak. His voice was soft and nice. "Far away from here," he said, "in a little street, there is a poor house." He spoke of a place that needed help.

A woman who makes clothes lives there. She is making beautiful flowers. These flowers are for a dress. The dress is for the Queen's servant. Her face is thin and tired from her work. Her red hands are hurt by the sharp tool she uses. This pain comes from her hard work. Her little boy is sick with a high temperature. He is asking for oranges to eat. But she has only river water to give to him. She has no money for fruit.

"Swallow, Swallow, little Swallow," said the Prince in a quiet voice. "Will you not bring her the red stone? It is from my weapon handle. My feet are stuck to this base, you see. I cannot move from here at all. If you could take the stone, it would help. This would make a big difference."

The Swallow answered the Prince. "My friends are now flying over the Nile river," he said. "Soon they will sleep in the resting place of a great King. His body is there, in a quiet room. The King is in his bright box for the dead. He is covered in yellow material from head to foot. Around his neck is a neck chain. It is made of light green stone. I should be with them."

"Swallow, Swallow, little Swallow," said the Prince once more. "Will you not stay with me for just one night? Will you be my carrier of news? The little boy needs water very much. His poor mother is so sad about this. Please help them." The Swallow thought about this request. If he were to help, his journey would be delayed. He said, "I do not think I like boys very much. Last summer, two bad boys threw stones at me. This happened when I was by the river. They never hit me, of course. Swallows fly too well for that to happen."

### C1
Above the bustling city, the gilded statue of the Happy Prince perched on a lofty column, catching every passerby's glance. Its bronze robe shimmered in the morning light, while its smile, frozen in marble, seemed to promise everlasting joy. Villagers often paused beneath its shadow, murmuring admiration for the figure's flawless beauty. A wise mother, soothing her wailing infant, whispered, "Be like the Happy Prince; he never weeps." A dour man, whose own heart felt heavy, muttered, "At least someone in this world knows true happiness."

When the charity children filed out of the modest church, their voices rose in a chorus, "He looks like a guardian spirit." Their teacher, a stern man with furrowed brows, scowled, disapproving of such fanciful speech. He believed imagination dulled the mind, and he warned the pupils to focus on practical lessons. Nevertheless, the children lingered, their eyes still fixed on the distant statue, hoping its silence concealed kindness. A faint breeze stirred the churchyard, carrying the faint scent of lilacs that seemed to echo the children's hope.

That night, a lone Swallow, its feathers dusted with the amber of distant suns, swooped over the sleeping city. It had lagged behind its flock, which had departed for Egypt weeks earlier, drawn by a lingering affection for a reed. The reed, a slender green blade standing by the river, had first captivated the Swallow during the fresh bloom of spring. Its delicate sway in the wind had seemed a promise of tenderness, and the bird had spent the warm summer beside it. Other Swallows, now soaring toward warmer climes, scoffed at his choice, calling it a foolish devotion to a penniless plant.

When autumn slipped its golden fingers across the landscape, the flock vanished, leaving the solitary Swallow to confront the chilling silence. He grew weary of the reed's quiet companionship, noting that her rustling offered no conversation, only the wind's fleeting jokes. "She only plays with the breeze," he muttered, his voice trembling like a leaf caught in a gust. He confessed to the reed, "I long to travel, to see beyond these riverbanks; perhaps you might share that yearning." When he finally asked, "Will you leave with me?", the reed swayed sadly and whispered, "My roots hold me here."

The Swallow's heart clenched, and a bitter tear rolled down his cheek as he cried, "You break my heart!" Resolute yet sorrowful, he spread his wings and declared, "I must fly toward the distant pyramids, even if you stay." He lifted off, feeling the chill of the evening air, and turned his back on the river's mournful lullaby. The reed rustled once more, as if sighing, while the sun slipped below the horizon, painting the sky with bruised purples. A cold wind brushed his feathers, urging him onward toward an unknown destiny beyond the familiar reeds.

He journeyed across endless plains, over bustling towns, and beneath starlit skies, his mind turning over memories of home. When night fell, he hovered above the city, searching for a perch where he could rest his weary wings. His eyes fell upon the radiant statue, its golden surface gleaming even in the moon's pale light. "That will be my bedroom," he chirped delightedly, imagining the lofty column as a lofty sanctuary. He settled between the statue's stone feet, feeling the cool marble beneath his claws as a gentle cradle.

Just as he tucked his head under a wing, a solitary drop of water struck his back, startling him. He looked upward, bewildered, for the sky was clear, the stars glittering like distant diamonds, yet a tear fell. "What strange weather is this?" he cried, his voice echoing against the cold stone, as another droplet landed. He muttered, "What use is a statue that cannot shield even a single bird from a single drop?" Frustrated, he prepared to fly away, but a third droplet fell, glistening like a tiny crystal.

He glanced up at the statue's face and saw the Prince's eyes brimming with luminous tears that streamed down gold cheeks. A pang of sorrow struck the Swallow, and he whispered, "Who are you, noble figure, that weeps in silence?" The statue answered in a voice as soft as a distant hymn, "I am the Happy Prince, once alive in the palace of Sans-Souci." He continued, "In life I knew no sorrow; now, fixed above this town, I witness its endless pain and cannot help but weep." The Swallow, humbled, thought silently, "Is gold truly capable of feeling such grief?" but kept his reverent curiosity to himself.

He pointed with a gentle gesture toward a narrow street, where a modest cottage housed a seamstress laboring over a single dress. Her thin hands were stained with red thread, and the needle she wielded left tiny bruises on her skin. Beside her, a small boy shivered with fever, his lips dry, pleading silently for a sweet orange that he could not afford. Only a cup of river water lay on the table, a stark reminder of their poverty. The Prince's voice softened further, "Swallow, could you fetch the ruby from my sword-hilt and bring it to the seamstress?"

The Swallow replied, "My companions now glide over the Nile, where a great king lies in a luminous sarcophagus, wrapped in golden shrouds." He described the king's emerald necklace, its green stones catching the dim light of the burial chamber. Yet the Prince implored, "Stay with me this night, deliver water to the child, and bring comfort to the grieving mother." The Swallow hesitated, recalling a summer incident when mischievous boys had thrown stones at him, though none had struck his swift body. He confessed, "I am not fond of boys; their cruel games leave me uneasy, yet I cannot ignore this plea."

Moved by the Prince's sorrow and the child's desperate need, the Swallow resolved to brave the night and fetch the ruby. He lifted his tiny heart, feeling both the weight of duty and the whisper of his own longing for adventure. With one final glance at the shining statue, he spread his wings, the cool rain now a gentle blessing. The city below glimmered under the moon, and the statue's golden tears fell like small suns upon the cobblestones. Thus began a night of quiet generosity, where a tiny bird and a silent prince together eased the suffering of strangers.

## st-0011 — The Adventures of Sherlock Holmes
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
It is a late night. I visit my friend Sherlock Holmes. He sits in his room. He does not feel well. But I am back in London. Now he feels very good.

He asks about my life. He asks what I do. I talk about my work. I work to help people. I have a new family now. He hears me well. He says I look very well.

Then he talks about his life. He works on difficult cases. He still loves his work. He likes to find bad people. He wants to understand the problems.

I see his great work. He sees very small details. Other people do not see the things. He uses details to find answers.

He shows me a letter. He receives it today. Someone wants to meet my friend. The person does not write a name. But the person has a big problem. It is a very important problem.

Holmes thinks about the letter. He wants to study this case. It is a very interesting case. He asks me to stay. He asks me to help.

I agree to stay with Holmes. We wait for the person. We want to start our work. We wait in the room.

The person comes at seven forty five. The person has a long black cover. The person has a cover on his head. The person looks very concerned.

Holmes speaks to the person. He asks the person to sit. He asks the person to explain. The person starts to talk. We hear the story with care.

Holmes writes notes during the talk. He asks many questions. He is very focused on this. I see his mind at work. He is thinking about the answer.

### A2
It was a cold evening in London. I went to see my friend Sherlock Holmes. He was sitting in his seat. He looked like he wanted to sleep. But I told him I was back in London. Then, he had more energy. He was very happy to see me. He sat up in his seat.

He asked me about my life. He wanted to know what I did. I told him about my work as a doctor. I also told him about my new wife. He listened to me with great care. He said I looked well and happy. He was happy I had a good life.

Then he talked about his own life. He said he was working on difficult cases. He was still very interested in police work. He loved to study bad things people did. He wanted to help people with their problems. His work was very important to him.

I was always surprised by my friend. He had a great ability to see details. He could see very small things. Other people did not see these details. But Holmes saw everything in the room. He used these small details to find answers. He could solve any difficult problem.

He showed me a letter. He had received it that day. The letter was from a person. The person wanted to meet him that night. The letter did not have a name. But it said the problem was very important. They needed to talk about it soon.

Holmes was very interested in this letter. He thought it was a great case. He was going to find the answer. He asked me to stay with him. He wanted me to help him that night. I was happy to help my friend.

I agreed to stay and help my friend. We sat down to wait for the person. We waited in his room. When the person arrived, we would start. We would work together on the case. We sat and talked for some time.

The person arrived at seven forty-five that night. They wore long black clothes and a large hat. They did not want us to see their face. They looked very worried. They also had a lot of fear. They stood near the door for a moment.

Holmes welcomed the person into the room. He asked them to sit in a chair. Then he asked them to explain their problem. The person started to speak. We listened to their words with great care. We wanted to understand everything they said.

The person spoke, and Holmes took many notes. He asked some short questions. He was very focused on the story. He did not look away. I could see his mind was working. He was already thinking about the answer. He was going to solve this case soon.

### B1
It was a very cold winter evening. I decided to visit my dear friend, Sherlock Holmes. I walked through the dark streets of London to reach his house. When I entered, he was sitting in his large chair. He looked very tired from his work. However, I told him that I was back in London. As soon as he heard this, he became much more active. He seemed very happy to see me after such a long time.

Holmes immediately began to ask me about my life. He wanted to know what I had been doing recently. I told him about my busy work as a doctor. I also told him about my wonderful new wife. He listened to my words very carefully. After I finished, he looked at me with a warm smile. He said that I looked very happy and healthy. He was glad that my life was going so well.

Then, Holmes started to talk about his own life. He had been working on some very difficult cases recently. He told me that his work was the most important thing to him. He was still extremely interested in crime and solving mysteries. If he does not have a difficult problem, he gets bored. He needs to use his mind all the time. He said that the city was full of mysteries.

I was always amazed by his wonderful ability. He could notice the smallest details about a person or a place. He saw things that other people completely missed. Then, he used those details to solve the most difficult mysteries. I have seen him do this many times before. Yet, it always surprised me. He could look at a coat and know where the person had been.

Holmes reached into his pocket and took out a letter. He showed it to me and asked me to read it. The letter had arrived earlier that day. It was from someone who wanted to meet with him tonight. The writer did not give their name in the letter. However, they wrote that they had a very important problem to discuss. It was clear that the writer was important.

Holmes was very interested in this strange letter. He thought it might become a highly interesting case for him. He asked me to stay and help him with this new mystery. He said, "If you stay, we will solve this together." I was very excited by his offer. I had missed our adventures together. It felt wonderful to be ready to work with him again.

I quickly agreed to stay and help him. We decided to wait together for the strange person to arrive. We sat by the warm fire and talked. I knew that when the guest arrived, we would start our new investigation. The clock on the wall made a slow, quiet sound. We both looked at the door, expecting to hear a knock at any moment.

Finally, the person arrived at exactly seven forty-five in the evening. We heard a horse stop outside the house. Then, there was a loud knock on the door. The guest entered our room wearing a long coat and a hat. The person looked very afraid and worried. They looked around the room quickly, as if they were afraid of something.

Holmes welcomed the visitor warmly and asked them to sit down. He tried to make the guest feel more comfortable in the room. Then, he asked them to explain their difficult problem to us. The person began to talk in a worried voice. We both sat quietly and listened very carefully to every word they said.

As the visitor spoke, Holmes took notes on a piece of paper. He also asked a few sharp questions to understand the details. He was extremely focused and concentrated on the story. I could see that his active mind was already working. He was already thinking about how to solve this difficult case. This was the beginning of another great adventure.

### B2
It was a cold winter evening when I visited my friend, Sherlock Holmes. The streets of London were covered in thick white snow. I walked to his house and went up the stairs to his room. He was sitting in his usual chair by the warm fire, looking very tired. A small light was burning on the table beside him. His face was pale, and he had been thinking deeply for many hours. I could see that he needed a new problem to focus his mind.

But when I told him that I was back in London, he became much happier. His dark eyes shone with a sudden light, and he smiled. He immediately asked me about my life and what I had been doing. I told him about my busy work as a doctor, which took up most of my time. I also spoke about my new wife, who made me very happy. He listened to everything with great interest.

He looked at me carefully for a few moments. Then he said that I looked both happy and healthy. He also noticed some wet ground on my boots, which showed that I had been walking in the rain. He said that if I had not been working so hard, my boots would be cleaner. Then he started to talk about his own life. He explained that he had been working on several difficult cases.

He told me that he was still very interested in crime and his special work. I was amazed by his extraordinary ability to notice small details. He could easily see things that other people missed. If he looked at a man, he could tell their whole story. He could use those small details to solve the most difficult mysteries.

He showed me a letter which had been received by him earlier that day. The letter was written on very strong paper that came from a foreign country. Holmes explained that the paper itself was an important sign. He said that if we examined the paper, we would find its origin. The person who wrote the letter did not give their name.

However, the writer said they had a very important problem to discuss. The letter was written in a strange way, which made Holmes very interested. He thought it might be a very interesting case to solve. He asked me if I would stay and help him with it. I agreed to stay because I always enjoyed our work together.

We waited patiently for the mysterious person to arrive. The fire was burning quietly, and the room was very still. Finally, at exactly seven forty-five in the evening, we heard a vehicle stop outside. A moment later, the door was opened, and our visitor entered. They were dressed in a long coat and a dark hat.

The visitor looked very nervous and worried as they stood before us. Their face was partly covered by their hat, which made them look even more strange. Holmes greeted them warmly and asked them to sit down in a comfortable chair. He told them that they could speak freely in front of me. He said that I was his most trusted friend.

The person began to explain their situation, and we listened carefully. They spoke about a secret relationship that had occurred some years ago. A photograph had been taken of them with another person, and now they were being threatened. The photograph was very important because it could ruin their reputation. They really needed Holmes to find this photograph and save them.

As the visitor spoke, Holmes took detailed notes and asked many questions. He was extremely focused and did not miss a single word. I could see that his brilliant mind was already working on a plan. He was a very smart man, and I was happy to be involved in this search. I knew that we would do everything possible to help our visitor.

Our investigation began the very next morning. We had to wear different clothes so that people would not recognize us. Holmes was a master of using different appearances to gather information. We went to the house where the photograph was believed to be hidden. We watched the building carefully for several hours to learn the habits of the owner.

Holmes created a smart plan to enter the house. He said that if we caused a small accident, we would be able to get inside. I was told to throw a small object through the window when he gave the sign. This would create a sudden cry of fire, which would frighten the people in the house.

The plan was carried out perfectly. When the cry of fire was heard, the woman who lived there ran to save her most valuable thing. In moments of danger, a person will always look at what they want to protect most. This action showed Holmes exactly where the secret photograph was hidden. He did not take it immediately, but we now knew its location.

The next day, we returned to the house to get the photograph. However, when we arrived, we discovered that the woman had already left the country. She had realized that Holmes was investigating her. She left a different photograph of herself and a letter for Holmes. In her letter, she promised that she would never use the secret photograph against our visitor.

We immediately informed our visitor about what had happened. He was very happy with this result because he knew he was finally safe. He thanked Holmes and me warmly for our help. Although we did not get the original photograph, the danger was completely gone. The visitor was able to return to his life without any more fear.

It was a very good experience for me. I was glad to have been able to work with Holmes on such an interesting case. I learned a lot from him during our time together. He was a very smart man who had a unique way of looking at the world. I was happy to have him as my friend, and I knew I would never forget our adventures.

### C1
On a bitter winter night, I made my way through London's snow-laden streets toward 221B Baker Street. The city lay hushed beneath a thick mantle of white, each lamplit window a lone beacon against the gloom. I ascended the narrow staircase, the wooden steps creaking softly under my boots as I approached the familiar door. Inside, Holmes occupied his usual armchair beside the fire, a wan light flickering on the table beside him. His pale face bore the marks of long contemplation, and I sensed a yearning for a fresh puzzle to occupy his keen mind.

When I announced my return to London, a sudden spark ignited in Holmes's dark eyes, and his lips curved into a rare smile. He immediately turned his attention to me, asking about the years that had passed since our last meeting. I spoke of my demanding medical practice, the long hours in the ward, and the joy brought by my new wife. He listened with an intensity that made me feel every word mattered, his eyebrows lifting at each detail. After a moment of quiet observation, he remarked that I appeared both vigorous and content, noting the dampness clinging to my boots.

Holmes then confessed that his recent days had been consumed by a series of perplexing crimes that tested even his formidable intellect. He emphasized his fascination with the minutiae that escape ordinary eyes, insisting that each trivial clue carries a hidden narrative. To illustrate, he said that a single glance at a man could reveal his profession, habits, and recent anxieties. Such observations, he explained, allow him to reconstruct the most tangled mysteries from fragments that others dismiss as inconsequential. He lifted a sealed envelope from the table, its thick foreign paper already a testament to the enigma it contained.

The envelope, embossed with an unfamiliar seal, had arrived earlier that afternoon, and Holmes examined its texture with reverent curiosity. He argued that the paper's grain and watermark could betray its country of origin, a clue often overlooked by careless correspondents. Inside lay a handwritten note, the author deliberately omitting any name, yet pleading for urgent assistance with a matter of grave importance. The script was irregular, each line uneven, which intrigued Holmes even more, suggesting a nervous hand perhaps concealing a secret. He asked me to remain, promising that together we might untangle the mystery, and I gladly consented, eager for the familiar intellectual chase.

We settled into a patient silence, the fire's crackle the only sound, as the clock ticked toward the appointed hour. At precisely seven forty-five, a carriage halted before the door, its wheels sighing against the snow-packed pavement. The visitor emerged, cloaked in a long coat and a low-brimmed hat, their shoulders trembling with concealed anxiety. Holmes greeted them warmly, gesturing toward a comfortable armchair, and assured them that I, his most trusted companion, would listen in confidence. The stranger lowered their head, the hat shadowing half a face, and began to recount a tale of a photograph that threatened to ruin their reputation.

They explained that years earlier they had entered a clandestine liaison, and a single image captured that indiscretion, now wielded by a blackmailer. The photograph, hidden for decades, could be extracted from a locked study belonging to a reclusive woman who guarded it jealously. Holmes listened intently, his pen moving in swift, precise strokes as he recorded each fragment of the narrative. His eyes never left the speaker, and his occasional questions revealed a mental map already forming, aligning motives, opportunities, and possible alibis. I sensed his mind assembling a stratagem, each detail a brick in a structure that would soon support the solution.

At dawn we prepared our disguises, Holmes insisting that anonymity was essential lest the suspect recognize the inquisitive eyes of the famed detective. He instructed me to adopt the attire of a modest clerk, while he himself would assume the guise of a curious merchant. Our target was a townhouse on a quiet lane, its facade unremarkable, yet rumors whispered of a hidden compartment within its walls. We observed the residence for several hours, noting the routines of its occupants, the times they entered and exited, and the subtle patterns of their movements. Holmes then proposed a delicate ruse: a staged fire alarm would draw the lady from her study, exposing the precise location of the concealed photograph.

When the signal was given, I hurled a small stone through a cracked window, the shattering glass echoing like a startled bird. The sudden crash provoked a shrill cry, and within moments the woman rushed to extinguish the imagined flame, clutching a leather-bound box. She placed the box upon a nearby table, its lid slightly ajar, revealing a faint outline of a photograph beneath a layer of dust. Holmes observed the scene with his characteristic calm, noting the exact position of the box and the direction of the wind that might carry scent. He refrained from taking the picture immediately, preferring instead to confirm the location before returning under the cover of night.

The following morning we arrived at the townhouse, only to discover that the lady had departed for a foreign shore, her absence confirmed by a hastily posted notice. In her place lay a second photograph, a portrait of herself, accompanied by a brief letter addressed to Holmes. She pledged that the original image would never be used to blackmail her former lover, and she promised to destroy it if ever it resurfaced. Holmes read the letter aloud, his voice tinged with admiration for the woman's unexpected courage and the cleverness of her final move. We relayed the news to our client, whose relief was palpable, for the specter of disgrace had finally been lifted.

The case concluded without the retrieval of the original photograph, yet its resolution afforded the visitor a clean slate and restored peace. I reflected on the evening's events, grateful for the privilege of collaborating with a mind that perceives the world as an intricate tapestry of clues. Holmes's ability to extract meaning from the subtlest of details reaffirmed my belief that observation, coupled with tenacity, is the true engine of discovery. Our friendship, forged in countless investigations, has become a lighthouse that guides me through the darkest of mysteries. I know that the adventures we share will linger in memory, their echoes resonating long after the fire has faded.

## st-0012 — Alice's Adventures in Wonderland
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
Alice falls down a long way. The way is black. She feels very bad. It is very black here. Alice does not know what to do.

Then she sees a big table. A very small thing is on it. Alice wants to open with this thing. She feels good to find this thing.

Alice finds a new, small part. This part is not open. She uses the small thing to open. Now the part is open. The open part is very small.

Alice wants to go through the part. But she is too big. She does not know what to do. Alice feels very bad now.

Alice wants to be very small. Then she can go through the part. She can see the other side. This is a great new time. It is for Alice.

Alice thinks about her life. She thinks of her family. She thinks of her good friends. And her good Dinah. She thinks of them much.

Alice hears a big sound. It is a something that can run. This something runs very well. It says, "I am very late!"

Alice follows the something that runs. She goes through a long, black way. The way is very black. She hears the something again.

Alice comes to a big, good room. The room is very good. Many parts are in the room. All the parts are not open.

Alice feels very bad now. She walks for a very long time. She wants to find a way out. She does not know how to get out.

### A2
Alice fell down a deep place. It was very dark inside. She did not know where she was. Alice felt much fear. She thought about her family. She thought about her home. She wanted to be good.

Then Alice saw a table. The table was made of clear material. A small key of yellow material was on the table. Alice looked at the key. She was happy to find it. She picked up the key. It felt cold in her hand.

Alice walked around the room. She found a small door. The door was very low. She tried to open it. The door was not open. Alice thought about the key. She used the small key of yellow material. She put the key in the door part. The door opened not fast.

Alice looked through the door. She saw a beautiful garden. It had many garden plants with colors. She wanted to go inside. But Alice was too big. She tried to go through. Her head touched the door. She could not pass. Alice felt not happy.

Alice wanted to be small. Then she could go into the garden. She could see all the garden plants. It was going to be a great time. She wanted to have new things. This was a new place for her. She wanted to see everything.

Alice thought about her home. She missed her family very much. She missed her good friends. She also missed her pet animal, Dinah. Dinah was a nice pet animal. Alice wanted to go home. But she was in a deep place. She did not know the way out.

Then Alice heard a voice. It was near her. She looked around quickly. A white animal ran past. He wore small blue clothes. The white animal was moving fast. He said, "Oh my! I am late!" He looked at his watch.

Alice followed the white animal fast. She ran after him. He went through a place. Alice went into the place too. She went through a long way. The way was very dark. She could not see much. But she could hear the white animal. She heard his small feet.

Alice came to a big room. It was a very big room. The room was very nice. There were many doors. All the doors were not open. Alice tried to open them. She pushed each door. But they did not open.

Alice had much need for rest now. She walked for a long time. She wanted to find a way out. She wanted to rest. But she did not know how. Where was she going to go? She felt very lost.

### B1
Alice fell down a very deep hole. She went down fast. She was very afraid inside it. The space was dark. Alice did not know what to do next. She had never been in such a dark place before. This new place made her feel very small. She looked all around her. But she could see nothing at all. She felt quite lost in this place. She wanted to go back up.

Then, Alice saw a small table. It stood there in the dark. The table was made of glass. It was clear and bright. On the table, there was a tiny gold key. Alice felt very happy to find this key. She had hope again. She thought the key could help her. She believed it would help her leave this strange place. She picked up the key. She held it with her small hand. This key was her only hope.

Alice started to look for a door. She walked around the dark room. Soon, she found a small door. It was low on the wall. The door was locked tightly. Alice used her new gold key. She put the key in the lock. The key fit the lock perfectly. She had a good feeling. She turned the key. The lock made a sound. She then opened the door. But the door was very, very small. It was just a little opening in the wall. She looked inside the opening.

Alice wanted to go through the door. She truly wanted to see. She wished to know what was outside. But she was much too big to pass through. Her head could not even fit inside. Alice did not know what to do now. She felt very sad about this problem. She had worked to find a way out. But she could not use it at all. This made her feel bad. There was no clear path for her.

Alice wished she could be small. She closed her eyes for a moment. If she can be small, she will go through. Then she will see what is on the other side. This will be a great new experience for her. She has always wanted a big new journey. She has wished for a special trip. She wants to find a new world. This idea made her feel a bit better.

Alice thought about her own life. She thought about her family. She also thought about her friends. She remembered her dear cat, Dinah. Alice missed them very much. She had left them far above the ground. She hoped to see them again soon. She felt a little alone now. This was a hard feeling. She was far from her home. She wished for their company.

Suddenly, Alice heard a voice. A white animal spoke very fast. It was a white animal. It had bright red eyes. The animal was moving very fast. It seemed to be in a rush. He said, "Oh dear! I am very late!" He had to go quickly from there. Alice was very surprised. She heard an animal talk. This was a truly strange sight.

Alice decided to follow the white animal. She moved fast after it. She went through a long passage. The passage was very dark. It was also very narrow. But she could still hear the white animal's voice. He was always saying he was late. Alice followed the sound of his fast feet. She went deeper into the dark.

Then Alice came to a big hall. The hall was very nice. It was also very long. There were many doors here. They were on every side of the hall. But all the doors were locked. Alice had not found an open door yet. She had looked at each one. She needed to find a new key now. Without it, she could not pass.

Alice felt very tired now. She had walked for a long time. Her feet were tired. She wanted to find a way out of here. But she did not know how to leave. She looked at all the locked doors. She felt she did not know what to do next. Alice just wanted to go home. She wanted to be back with Dinah. She hoped to find her way soon.

Then she saw a table. On the table, there was a small key. The key was gold.

She found a door. The door was locked. She used the key to open the door.

The door was very small. She wanted to go through the door. But she was too big.

But all the doors were locked. She was very tired. She had been walking for a long time.

### C1
The earth gave way beneath Alice's feet, and she tumbled into a yawning abyss that swallowed the daylight whole. A cold, oppressive darkness pressed against her skin, making every breath feel like inhaling the night itself. She pressed her palms to the damp stone, searching for any hint of texture that might guide her next move. A faint echo of her own voice returned, distorted and distant, as if the cavern itself were listening. She felt a tremor of panic rise, yet a thin thread of curiosity tugged her forward.

After stumbling through the darkness, her eyes caught a faint glimmer: a solitary wooden table illuminated by a stray shaft of light. On its surface lay a tiny, intricately wrought key, its metal gleaming like a captured sunbeam. The key's golden hue seemed out of place in the cavern's gloom, as though it belonged to a royal chest. She lifted it gingerly, feeling its cool weight settle in her palm, and a faint hope sparked within her. The key, though small, seemed to promise an answer to the mystery that surrounded her.

Further ahead, a massive oak door loomed, its iron hinges rusted but still firmly anchored to the stone wall. A heavy bolt barred its entrance, and the keyhole glinted invitingly, matching the golden key she now held. She slipped the key into the lock, feeling the familiar click as tumblers aligned under her tentative turn. The door creaked open just enough to reveal a narrow passage, its darkness deeper than the abyss she had just escaped. A rush of stale air brushed her face, carrying the faint scent of old wood and distant rain.

Beyond the opening stood a tiny wooden door, no larger than a child's wardrobe, its paint chipped by time. Alice pressed her forehead against the frame, realizing with a pang that her own shoulders dwarfed the portal. The yearning to slip through and discover what lay beyond swelled within her, yet her body refused to shrink. A hot tear traced her cheek, and she whispered to the empty corridor, 'If only I could be small.' The echo of her own words returned, a reminder that the world often offers doors without the key to fit them.

She sank onto a cold stone ledge, and memories of her garden, her laughing siblings, and Dinah the cat flooded her mind. The soft purr of Dinah, once a comforting lullaby, seemed now a distant melody echoing through the cavern walls. She imagined her mother's warm hands brushing her hair, a gesture that anchored her to safety whenever fear rose. A sudden ache settled in her chest, not merely for adventure, but for the simple joy of returning home. Yet the promise of an unknown realm tugged at her curiosity, urging her to press on despite the ache.

From the far end of the passage, a hurried voice cut through the stillness, its tone sharp and breathless. A man shouted something about being late, his words tumbling like frantic beads across the stone. Instinctively, Alice followed the sound, her steps echoing louder as she drew nearer to the source. The corridor narrowed, and the air grew cooler, carrying whispers of distant conversations and faint metallic clangs. She felt a strange mixture of anticipation and dread, as if she were about to cross a threshold between worlds.

The passage opened onto a grand hall, its vaulted ceiling soaring high above rows of marble pillars bathed in soft amber light. Each wall was lined with ornate doors, their brass knobs polished to a mirror shine, yet every lock lay stubbornly sealed. Alice's footsteps echoed, creating a rhythmic pattern that seemed to pulse in time with her racing heart. She felt the weight of exhaustion settle into her bones, each breath a reminder of the miles she had traversed. Yet the splendor of the hall sparked a flicker of hope, for such majesty could not belong to a place of emptiness.

At the far end, a plush armchair beckoned, its deep cushions inviting her weary frame to rest. She sank into the soft fabric, feeling the tension ease as the chair seemed to cradle her like a mother. Around her, a bustling crowd moved with purpose, their murmurs blending into a low hum that filled the space. They were engrossed in their own affairs, scarcely noticing the newcomer who sat quietly amid their choreographed dance. A gentle smile brushed her lips, for in that moment she felt a fleeting connection to the invisible tapestry of lives.

Summoning courage, Alice rose and approaches a small group, hoping a simple greeting might bridge the silence. Her voice trembled as she introduced herself, but the hurried conversations around her drowned her words like rain. She retreated, cheeks flushed, and wandered the hall's periphery, searching for a sign that might guide her onward. Every door she examined bore the same unyielding lock, each rejection sharpening the ache of disappointment. A sigh escaped her lips, mingling with the distant echo of footsteps that seemed to promise an answer.

From the shadows emerged the hurried man, his coat slightly disheveled, yet his eyes held a warm, welcoming light. He offered a courteous smile, and when Alice explained her plight, he chuckled softly, as if recalling an old joke. 'It is but a test,' he said, tapping the locked doors, 'and you have already shown the perseverance needed to pass.' He gestured toward a grand archway beyond the hall, its threshold bathed in golden light, inviting her to step forward. With a sudden surge of confidence, Alice followed him, feeling the weight of the test lift from her shoulders.

The archway opened onto an expansive chamber, its walls adorned with vibrant tapestries depicting scenes of distant lands and joyous gatherings. Around a long table sat a circle of smiling faces, each person eager to share stories and offer guidance. Alice was welcomed with open arms, and as the evening unfolded, she absorbed lessons of kindness, curiosity, and resilience. She formed bonds with strangers who became friends, their laughter weaving a comforting blanket over the lingering loneliness. Time slipped by unnoticed, and when the sun's first rays filtered through stained glass, she sensed that her stay must end.

With a grateful heart, Alice bid farewell to her new companions, promising to carry their teachings back to her own world. The journey home felt both familiar and strange, each step echoing the lessons learned within the hall's enchanted walls. She reunited with her family, her mother's warm embrace and Dinah's soft purr grounding her after the surreal adventure. When she recounted the tale, her listeners listened with awe, their eyes reflecting the wonder she had once felt. Now, years later, she often gazes at the door in her garden, remembering the test, the man, and the hall that taught her that courage can be found in every hidden passage.

## st-0013 — The Adventures of Tom Sawyer, Complete
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
Tom Sawyer is a child. He is a young child. He loves fun. He loves new things. He lives with Aunt Polly. They live in a small town. Tom always has problems. He is not always a good child.

One day, Tom runs away. He runs from his home. He does not feel good there. He takes a small water thing. He goes to some land. This land is on water. He feels free now.

Tom meets a child there. His name is Huck Finn. Huck has no money. He is a child. He lives outside. He does not have a home. Huck is also a young child. Tom and Huck talk.

Tom and Huck become friends. They have much great fun. They do new things. They go together. They look for money. They look for some money. They look at the land. They see new things. The land is their new home.

Their fun has problems. They must watch. They stay away from law. They stay away from bad people. Bad people are in town. These people are very bad. Tom and Huck must run.

Tom and Huck learn many things. They learn about friends. They learn to help friends. They learn to be true. This is important for them. They grow up a little. They understand many things.

In the end, Tom goes home. He goes to Aunt Polly. He feels good there. He is back home now. He always remembers fun. He remembers new things. He always remembers Huck. His home is a good place. Aunt Polly feels good too.

Tom and Huck stay friends. They are best friends. They always help each other. New fun is coming. They are always together. They are strong friends. They are good together.

### A2
Tom Sawyer was a young boy. He lived with his Aunt Polly. Their home was in a small town. The town was by a big water. Tom was often in trouble. He did not like rules.

Tom did not like school much. He often ran away from school. He liked to play all day. He wanted to do fun things. He wanted to have a good time.

One day, Tom wanted to run away from home. He had a big idea. He took a small thing from the water bank. This thing was a small boat. He went to a big place in the water. The place was an island.

The place was in the water. Tom was going to live there alone. He wanted to be free. No one would tell him what to do. He did not want to work. He did not want to go to school.

On the place, Tom met Huck Finn. Huck was another young boy. Huck was very poor. He lived on the streets in town. He did not have a home or family. He was often alone.

Tom and Huck talked much. They liked each other right away. They wanted to have fun together. They became very good friends. They had many great times. They were happy boys.

Tom and Huck went to find something new. They looked for things all over the place. They saw new things. They played many games every day. They were very happy on the place.

There were some bad people. Tom and Huck knew this. They must be very good. They needed to avoid the law. Police were in town. Bad people were also there. Tom and Huck wanted to be safe.

Tom and Huck learned new and good things. They learned about being true. They learned to be good boys. They learned to help each other. These things were very important. They remembered them well.

In the end, Tom went back home. He saw his Aunt Polly again. She was very happy to see him. She gave him a big hug. Tom was happy to be home. He loved his Aunt Polly very much.

Tom and Huck stayed good friends. They were the best of friends. They would always help each other. This was very important for them. More fun times were coming for them.

They would be together for many years. They would live many more stories. Tom would always remember his friend. Huck was a good friend to Tom. Tom was happy to have Huck as a friend. Their friendship was strong.

### B1
Tom Sawyer was a young boy. He loved to find new adventures. Tom lived in a small town. He lived with his Aunt Polly. Aunt Polly tried to keep him safe. But Tom often found trouble. He liked to play and explore. He did not always follow the rules. Sometimes, Aunt Polly felt tired. She worried about Tom very much. Tom often wanted to do his own thing. He liked to discover new places. He did not want to stay at home. He often thought about faraway lands.

One day, Tom was very unhappy. He felt that no one understood him. He made a big decision. Tom decided to run away from his home. He needed to find a new place. He wanted a place for new adventures. Tom found a small boat. He took the boat to the river. He put the boat in the water. Then he sailed to a nearby island. The island was in the middle of the river. He thought this place was perfect. It was a good place to be free. He had never been so far before. He was excited for what was next.

On the island, Tom was not alone. He soon met another boy there. The boy's name was Huck Finn. Huck was a poor child. He did not have a home. He lived on the streets of the town. Huck did not go to school. He learned about life in a different way. Tom and Huck talked for a long time. They quickly became good friends. They both loved to have fun. They both wanted excitement. Huck knew many interesting things. Tom liked Huck's free spirit. They felt like brothers.

Tom and Huck had many exciting adventures. They explored the whole island. They found new places to play. They climbed tall trees. They swam in the river every day. They also went on a treasure hunt. They looked for old gold and money. They thought they might find something great. They dug in many secret spots. It was hard work but also fun. They spent many days together. Their friendship grew very strong. They always helped each other. They felt like real explorers.

But their adventures had some danger. They had to be very careful. There were bad people in town. Tom and Huck needed to avoid them. The law also watched them. Sometimes, they did things that were not right. They did not want to get into real trouble. They had to hide sometimes. They learned to be smart. They learned to think fast. Danger was part of their new life. If they were not careful, they would lose their freedom. They learned to trust their instincts.

Through these experiences, they learned much. Tom and Huck learned many important lessons. They learned about being true friends. They learned about loyalty. This means always helping your friend. They also learned to be true to themselves. They understood their own value. They learned that good choices are important. They have grown up a lot. Their time on the island changed them. They now see the world differently. They understood what truly mattered.

Finally, it was time to go home. Tom decided to return to Aunt Polly. He missed his family. He missed his bed and good food. Aunt Polly was very happy to see him. She had worried about him so much. Tom was also happy to be back. But he would never forget the island. He would always remember Huck. He would remember all their great adventures. His heart was full of memories. He knew he had changed. Life at home felt different now.

Tom and Huck remained the best of friends. They shared a special bond. They always understood each other. They had faced danger together. They had also found joy. They knew they could trust each other. They would always help each other. They would have each other's backs. This means they would always support each other. If new adventures come, they will be ready. They would meet them together. Their friendship would last a long time. Nothing could break their bond.

### B2
Tom Sawyer was a boy who loved adventure. He lived with his Aunt Polly in a small town. Tom was always getting into trouble, seeking new fun. He often dreamed of places far from his quiet home. Aunt Polly tried hard to keep him in line. But Tom's spirit was free. Trouble often seemed to find him.

One day, Tom decided to run away from home. He felt a strong wish to go far away. He took a small boat and sailed to an island in the river. The sun was warm, and a gentle wind blew. He put some food and water in the boat. He sailed away from the town. The island was green, a perfect place for discovery.

Tom met a boy named Huck Finn on the island. Huck was a poor boy who lived on the streets. He had no real home and often slept outside. His clothes were old, but his eyes were bright. Huck knew much about the river and living free. Tom had never met anyone like him. They quickly became friends, sharing a love for adventure.

Tom and Huck became friends and had many exciting adventures together. Their days on the island were full of joy. They went on a hunt in the forest. They explored the island, finding old caves and hidden paths. They learned to work together well. Each new discovery felt great. They felt a strong bond growing.

But their adventures were not without danger. Sometimes, their fun had a serious side. There were bad people near the town. Tom and Huck had to watch out for them. The law also kept an eye on things. If they were not smart, they could get into trouble. They learned to think fast and make good choices. This made their friendship stronger.

Tom and Huck learned many valuable lessons on their adventures. These lessons were very important for them. They learned about friendship and being true to each other. They understood that a friend is always there. They saw that working together made them strong. Being true was a key part. This new understanding stayed with them.

In the end, Tom returned home to his Aunt Polly. She was very glad to see him safe. She had worried much about Tom. Tom told her some stories from the island. He did not tell her everything. His time there was special. He knew their friendship with Huck was real. He felt a new sense of calm.

Tom and Huck remained the best of friends. Their bond was not broken by distance. They often met for new games and shared more stories. Their friendship was a strong thing. They knew they could always depend on each other. If one had a problem, the other would help. This was a clear mark of their deep bond.

If Tom had not run away from home, he would not have met Huck. This choice changed his life. His time away led to much good. It opened up a new world for him. He was glad he took the chance to explore. Without that choice, a great friendship would not exist. He knew some risks lead to good rewards.

As a result, Tom and Huck had many exciting adventures together. They shared many secrets and faced small challenges. Each day brought new things to see. They became like brothers, sharing a deep bond. Their friendship lasted a very long time. They saw each other as family. Their bond was true for all their days.

Although Tom returned home, he never forgot about Huck. The memories of their adventures were clear in his mind. He often thought about his friend and their exciting times. The river, the island, and the forest came to his thoughts. These images made him smile. He knew he was lucky to have such a friend. He always kept Huck close.

If Tom and Huck had not been careful, they would have gotten into much trouble. But they were careful, and learned to plan well. They thought about what might happen before they acted. Their quick thinking helped them avoid the law. They also avoided the bad people in town. They learned the importance of being alert. Their goal was just to have fun.

Tom and Huck's adventures taught them many valuable lessons. They truly understood the meaning of trust. They knew how important it was to be honest. They also learned about courage. They found power in their shared spirit. These important ideas were part of who they were. They learned about friendship and being true to each other. Their experiences shaped them for the better.

They also learned to always have each other's backs. This sense of common purpose grew strong. No matter what adventures lay ahead, they would face them together. They knew they would always support each other. This was a deep promise between them. They could always count on their friend. This belief gave them much strength.

In the future, Tom and Huck would have many more adventures together. The future held much excitement for them. New places would be found, and new mysteries solved. Their bond would only grow stronger. They would always be there for each other. Their friendship would last a very long time. They had many big plans for later days.

The story of Tom and Huck is a classic tale of friendship and adventure. This tale is known by many people. It shows the strong power of two boys. Their choices shaped their world and their stories. It has been passed down for generations. It will continue to be told for many years to come. People often read about their fun. It brings a smile to many faces.

The story teaches us about the importance of friendship and being true to each other. From their tale, we learn many valuable things. We see how vital a good friend is. We learn to make smart choices. We should think carefully before we act. It also teaches us to be careful and to avoid trouble. The boys show us a good path. This tale holds much wisdom.

Tom and Huck's friendship is an example to us all. Such strong bonds are truly valuable in life. They give us much strength and joy. We all need people we can trust. We should all try to have friendships like theirs. Friendships that last a very long time and stay true. These two boys show us how to build such a bond. Their way of life is a good model.

In conclusion, the story of Tom and Huck is a story about friendship and adventure. It is a tale that touches the heart. It speaks of freedom, choice, and youth's fun. It reminds us of childhood dreams. It teaches us valuable lessons. It will continue to be told for many years to come. It is a story for everyone to enjoy. Its message is clear and strong.

### C1
Tom Sawyer, a wiry twelve-year-old with a restless imagination, lived under his stern yet caring Aunt Polly in the sleepy river town of St. Petersburg. The town's narrow cobblestone streets and the lazy flow of the Mississippi seemed to Tom both a cradle and a cage. Aunt Polly, whose prayers were as constant as the church bell, tried to tether his boundless energy with chores and sermons. Yet every night, when the lantern light flickered, Tom's thoughts drifted beyond the town's borders toward distant horizons he had never seen. In his heart, adventure pulsed like a drumbeat, urging him to break the ordinary and chase a taste of freedom.

One sultry afternoon, driven by an impulse he could not restrain, Tom slipped the back door and stole Aunt Polly's small wooden rowboat. He packed a modest bundle of bread, cheese, and a cracked water flask, whispering a promise to return before dusk. The river, warm and shimmering under the sun, welcomed his solitary craft as a gentle breeze coaxed the oars forward. Soon the familiar silhouette of the town receded, replaced by a veil of green reeds and the distant outline of an uninhabited island. When his boat brushed against the soft sand, Tom felt a thrilling shiver of triumph and a pang of solitude.

Exploring the island's tangled underbrush, Tom heard a rustle and turned to see a lanky boy perched on a fallen log. The boy's clothes were threadbare, his hair tangled, yet his eyes gleamed with a wild intelligence that pierced the afternoon haze. He introduced himself as Huckleberry Finn, a wanderer of the riverbanks who had learned to survive by listening to the water's whispers. Tom, drawn by the boy's fearless demeanor, felt an instant kinship, as if the river itself had guided them together. They exchanged stories of imagined treasure and whispered vows to explore every hidden corner of the island before night fell.

At dawn, the duo set out into the dense forest, their boots sinking into soft moss as the canopy filtered golden light. They followed the trail of a shy deer, learning to move silently, their breaths synchronised like a shared secret. Deeper within, they discovered a limestone cave whose walls glistened with mineral veins, echoing their laughter in its cool chambers. Inside, a narrow fissure led them to a hidden glade where wild berries grew in abundance, a feast for hungry wanderers. Their triumph was quiet, yet each discovery stitched a tighter bond between the boys, as if the island itself approved their camaraderie.

The island's serenity, however, was shadowed by rumors that a band of thieves used the river's bends to smuggle stolen goods. One evening, as the boys rested near a flickering fire, they heard distant shouts and the clatter of hooves approaching. A posse of town constables, alerted by the thieves' misdeeds, swept through the nearby marsh, their lanterns cutting the darkness like knives. Tom's heart hammered so fiercely that his ears seemed to ring, yet Huck's calm voice reminded him to move silently and hide. They slipped behind a thicket of willows, holding their breath as the search parties passed, their survival hinging on quick wit and quiet resolve.

When the night finally yielded to sunrise, Tom and Huck emerged from their hiding place, eyes bright with relief and newfound respect. They shared a simple breakfast of stale bread and river water, savoring each bite as if it were a treasure reclaimed. In that quiet moment, Tom realized that courage without a trusted companion could easily become reckless bravado. Huck, whose life had been a series of solitary nights, confessed that loyalty felt like a warm blanket against the cold of uncertainty. Their pact, forged in the island's wild heart, promised to guard each other's secrets and to face any threat together, come what may.

With the summer waning, Tom knew the time had come to sail back, his heart heavy yet hopeful for the familiar embrace of home. Aunt Polly, upon seeing his battered boat bobbing at the dock, rushed forward, tears glistening like dew on her apron. She scolded him gently for his reckless wanderings, yet her voice trembled with relief, betraying the depth of her love. Tom recounted the island's verdant glades and the echoing caves, omitting only the darker shadows that lingered beyond his tongue. Aunt Polly listened, her eyes softening as she sensed the subtle transformation that adventure had etched upon her nephew's soul.

Even after Tom's return, the river's whisper carried Huck's name to his ears, a reminder that true friendship endures beyond proximity. They met on weekends at the old dock, swapping tales of schoolyard mischief and secret maps drawn in charcoal. Each reunion sparked fresh plans to reconquer the island's forgotten trails, their laughter rippling across the water like wind-chimes. Their bond, tested by time and temptation, proved resilient, for they knew that reliance was the strongest armor against life's uncertainties. In quiet moments, Tom would glance toward the river's bend, feeling Huck's presence as an invisible compass guiding his choices.

Had Tom never fled that scorching afternoon, the serendipitous crossing with Huck would have remained a phantom, never materializing into reality. His world would have stayed confined to Aunt Polly's modest garden and the predictable rhythm of town gossip. Without the island's challenges, Tom might never have discovered the fierce loyalty that blossoms only when danger forces hearts to align. Thus, the daring decision to escape became the catalyst that reshaped his destiny, turning a solitary boy into a companion of equal daring. He learned that risk, when tempered with wisdom, can forge bonds stronger than any iron chain.

The years ahead promised more uncharted coves, hidden riddles, and the occasional brush with mischief, each awaiting the duo's intrepid spirit. Their stories would ripple through the town like folklore, inspiring other youths to seek wonder beyond the confines of their own yards. Yet, even as they chased new horizons, the island's emerald silhouette would remain a steadfast reminder of their first true partnership. In the quiet evenings, Tom would sit by his fire, recalling the echo of Huck's laughter, feeling gratitude for the path he once dared to forge. Thus, the legend of Tom and Huck endured, a timeless testament to the power of daring friendship and the endless allure of adventure.
